Reinforcement Learning for Respondent-Driven Sampling
Justin Weltz, Angela Yoon, Yichi Zhang, Alexander Volfovsky, Eric Laber

Abstract
Respondent-driven sampling (RDS) is widely used to study hidden or hard-to-reach populations by incentivizing study participants to recruit their social connections. The success and efficiency of RDS can depend critically on the nature of the incentives, including their number, value, call to action, etc. Standard RDS uses an incentive structure that is set a priori and held fixed throughout the study. Thus, it does not make use of accumulating information on which incentives are effective and for whom. We propose a reinforcement learning (RL) based adaptive RDS study design in which the incentives are tailored over time to maximize cumulative utility during the study. We show that these designs are more efficient, cost-effective, and can generate new insights into the social structure of hidden populations. In addition, we develop methods for valid post-study inference which are non-trivial due to the adaptive sampling induced by RL as well as the complex dependencies among subjects due to latent (unobserved) social network structure. We provide asymptotic regret bounds and illustrate its finite sample behavior through a suite of simulation experiments.



Social network data are at the forefront of healthcare research.
It is widely acknowledged that a better understanding of community social structure can provide insights into the prevalence of mental illnesses such as depression, transmittable diseases such as HIV, syphilis, and COVID, and other conditions like obesity and type-II diabetes
(
Ma et al., 2007
;
Tabák et al., 2012
;
Eisenberg et al., 2013
;
Perry et al., 2018
)
.
Currently, available methodologies for studying the underlying social network in a community fall broadly into two
categories: (1) complete community census
(
Mahmood et al., 2014
, such as in the Framingham Heart Study,)
, or (2) network sampling algorithms.
The magnitude of modern healthcare needs makes complete censuses nearly unachievable (and generally impractical) as the relevant sampling frames are usually unknown. Consequently, network sampling techniques have proven invaluable
(see
Raifman et al., 2022
, for a recent survey)
.

Respondent-driven sampling (RDS) is a network sampling algorithm based on participant referral which is frequently employed for surveillance in public health research
(
Heckathorn, 1997
;
Wejnert and Heckathorn, 2011
;
Heckathorn and Cameron, 2017
)
.
RDS begins with an initial group (usually a convenience sample) of individuals who are given a limited number of coupons and asked to recruit other members of the population of interest by giving them one of the coupons directly or by providing their contact information to the research team. Recipients of these initial coupons redeem them with study researchers; they are then compensated, interviewed, and given new coupons to recruit additional subjects. This process continues until
a sufficient sample is generated, the study budget or duration is reached, or
some other stopping criterion is met.

The attributes of the coupons, e.g., their number, value, call to action, expiration date, etc., can
play a critical role in shaping the evolution of the RDS process. However, in standard RDS,
researchers give each participant
an identical coupon allocation which is typically based on convention rather
than characteristics of the population under study

(
Goel and Salganik, 2010
)
. This is inefficient in that coupon return
rates, population coverage, and cost may be poor relative to what could be achieved with a tailored
coupon allocation. Despite being widely recognized,
attempts to address this issue have been scarce.

Lunagómez et al. (2018)
proposed calibrating the
number of coupons by simulating RDS under a parametric model and an
informative prior, but they do not consider adapting coupon allocations
to information accumulating during the study.
McFall et al. (2021)
ran a two-stage
RDS study in which an initial RDS study was used to identify
characteristics of participants likely to be successful recruiters, then
in a subsequent RDS study, participants with these characteristics were
given extra coupons
(see
VanOrsdale, 2023
, for an extensive simulation study of such
two-stage designs)
. Two-stage designs make use
of interim data but include only a single adaptation step and require
independent samples collected across two studies, which is often
impractical.

We use reinforcement learning
(
Sutton and Barto, 2018
, RL,)
to adapt coupon
allocations over time within a single RDS study in such a way that
some study objective is optimized. Example study objectives
include maximizing
information about disease prevalence and reaching as many individuals
in the target population as possible under budget and time
constraints. Our approach
uses a Markov branching process

(
Sevast’yanov and Zubkov, 1974
;
Athreya et al., 2004
)

as a working model to guide coupon allocation during the study. However,
for post-study inference, we do not assume that this model is correct.
Instead, we develop a novel projection confidence set that provides valid finite sample coverage for a large class of functionals of the generative model, even when the true model is not identifiable under RDS. This is non-trivial
as we are combining two procedures which are notorious for their
inferential challenges: (i)
RDS which is complicated by differential response probabilities, homophily, and other design effects

(
Goel and Salganik, 2010
;
Gile and Handcock, 2010
;
Tomas and Gile, 2011
;
Lu et al., 2012
;
Roch and Rohe, 2018
;
Rohe, 2019
)
; and (ii) adaptive
RL-based experimentation for which standard bootstrap or normality based inference
procedures can fail to provide nominal coverage

(
Deshpande et al., 2018
;
Zhang et al., 2020
;
Bibaut et al., 2021
;
Zhan et al., 2023
;
Bibaut and Kallus, 2024
)
. To address these complications, we develop valid post adaptive-experimentation inference procedures
for
M
-estimators constructed from Markov decision processes

(
Putterman, 1994
, MDPs,)
, a result which is of independent interest. In simulation experiments, our proposed procedure, which we term RL-RDS, significantly improves efficiency relative
to static and two-stage RDS designs. Furthermore, the projection confidence sets deliver nominal coverage without being excessively conservative.

The contributions of this work are summarized as follows:
(1) we develop the first principled framework for adaptive RDS using RL; (2) we show that a
Markov branching process approximation to the RDS process is useful for
guiding online adaptation;
(3) we prove regret bounds for our RL algorithm under the Markov branching process model; and (4) we develop valid finite sample inference
methods for adaptive-RDS without requiring identifiability.

In Section
1
, we review RDS. In Section
2
,
we introduce our branching process approximation and RL-based adaptive
coupon selection. In Section
2.1
, we present
regret bounds for the branching process approximation.
In Section
3
, we introduce our inference approach and prove that confidence regions constructed by this procedure achieve nominal coverage in finite samples and concentrate asymptotically.
Lastly, in Section
4
, we present
a suite of simulation experiments comparing RL-RDS, static, and two-stage designs.

1
Setup and Notation

An RDS study recruits participants in epochs or waves. The initial epoch,
which comprises individuals
\mathcal{E}_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
, is typically collected as a convenience sample. Generally, we use
i
to index interim study participants organized in epochs and
v
to index interim study participants ordered by arrival time.
Each participant
i\in\mathcal{E}_{0}
receives an allocation of coupons
{\mathbf{A}}_{i}\in\mathcal{A}

to distribute among
their social contacts. These coupons explain the study and encourage participation by offering an incentive. The data collected in the zeroth
epoch are thus

\mathcal{Z}_{0}\triangleq\left\{(T_{i},{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i},Y_{i},{\mathbf{A}}_{i},C_{i})\,:\,i\in\mathcal{E}_{0}\right\}
, where
T_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
is the arrival
time of participant
i
,
{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}\in\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}

are their covariates,

Y_{i}\in\mathcal{Y}\subseteq[0,1]
is an outcome of interest,
{\mathbf{A}}_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
is their coupon allocation, and
C_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
is the cost
of their recruitment. The set of individuals in epoch
j\geq 1
is
denoted by

\mathcal{E}_{j}\subseteq\mathbb{N}\setminus\left\{\cup_{j^{\prime}<j}\mathcal{E}_{j^{\prime}}\right\}
and their
associated data are
\mathcal{Z}_{j}\triangleq\left\{(R_{i},T_{i},{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i},Y_{i},{\mathbf{A}}_{i},C_{i})\,:\,i\in\mathcal{E}_{j}\right\}
, where
R_{i}\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}
denotes the recruiter of individual

i\in\mathcal{E}_{j}
. Individuals in each epoch are given coupons to recruit the next
epoch until available resources are depleted or another stopping criterion is met; for concreteness, we assume that the study terminates the
first time the total cost exceeds a fixed budget
D
.
Figure (
1
) illustrates the evolution of an RDS sample.

We note that the covariates
{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}
and the outcome
Y_{i}
may (though they
need not) be measured simultaneously. Even if this is the case,
Y_{i}
is
distinguished by its role in defining the adaptive RDS algorithm’s objective.
As detailed below, we define an optimal coupon allocation strategy as one that
maximizes the cumulative sum of the outcome across the sample.
For example, in an RDS
study of people who inject drugs, the outcome may be choosing to be tested for
HIV, and
participant attributes might include
demographic information,
PrEP use, history of STI testing, and attitudes and intentions related to risky
behaviors
(
Risser et al., 2009
)
.
In an RDS study targeting colorectal cancer
screening among non-utilizers of a healthcare
system, participant attributes might include
demographic information, family medical
history, previous FIT or colonoscopy screening,
and risk factors for colorectal cancer.
The outcome might be a participant’s
screening intention. Post-selection inference could focus on the
distribution of the covariates, the outcome, or both
(
Cooks et al., 2022
)
.

Let
J
denote
the total number of epochs. We use an overline to represent history so
that
\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{J}\triangleq\bigcup_{j\leq J}\mathcal{E}_{j}

are the complete sets of study participants,
and
\overline{\mathcal{Z}}_{J}\triangleq\bigcup_{j\leq J}\mathcal{Z}_{j}

are the data that correspond to the members of
\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{J}
.
For simplicity, we assume that subjects are processed
sequentially; i.e., no two individuals arrive at exactly
the same time. Therefore,
the data may be equivalently represented as
\mathcal{D}^{\kappa}\triangleq\left\{\left(R^{v},T^{v},{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{v},Y^{v},{\mathbf{A}}^{v},C^{v}\right)\right\}_{v=1}^{\kappa}
, in which individuals are indexed by their arrival times.

Under adaptive RDS, when an individual arrives in the study, they are assigned
a coupon allocation based on accumulated information on all prior participants.
Let
{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{v}\in\mathcal{H}^{v}
denote the information available to researchers
at the time the
v^{th}
subject
is given their coupon allocation
{\mathbf{A}}^{v}\in\mathcal{A}
.
For each
{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{v}={\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{v}
, let
\psi^{v}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{v})\subseteq\mathcal{A}

denote the set of allowable coupon combinations given
{\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{v}

(e.g., this set may
be restricted to ensure budget constraints are not violated).
A deterministic allocation strategy
\boldsymbol{\pi}=(\pi^{1},\pi^{2},\ldots)\in\Pi
is
a sequence of functions such that
\pi^{v}:\mathcal{H}^{v}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
and
\pi^{v}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{v})\in\psi^{v}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{v})
for all
{\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{v}
. The set
\Pi

can be restricted to
exclude allocation strategies
that are inherently unfair or harmful or to improve the tractability of finding the optimal allocation strategy.
We
define an optimal allocation strategy,
\boldsymbol{\pi}^{\mathrm{opt}}\in\Pi
, as maximizing
the expected value of the cumulative outcome across the RDS sample.

We formalize the optimal allocation strategy
within the potential outcomes framework
(
Rubin, 1978
;
Splawa-Neyman et al., 1990
)
.
For each
v\geq 2
, let
{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{v*}(\overline{{\mathbf{a}}}^{v-1})
denote the
potential history under the sequence of coupon allocations

\overline{{\mathbf{a}}}^{v-1}\triangleq({\mathbf{a}}^{1},{\mathbf{a}}^{2},\ldots,{\mathbf{a}}^{v-1})
. For
any deterministic allocation strategy
\boldsymbol{\pi}\in\Pi
, the potential history
at time
v\geq 2
is

{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{v*}(\boldsymbol{\pi})\triangleq\sum_{\overline{{\mathbf{a}}}^{v-1}}{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{v*}(\overline{{\mathbf{a}}}^{v-1})\prod_{k=1}^{v-1}\mathbb{I}\left[\pi^{k}\left\{{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{k*}(\overline{{\mathbf{a}}}^{k-1})\right\}={\mathbf{a}}^{k}\right],

where we have defined
{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{1*}({\mathbf{a}}^{0})\equiv{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{1}
and

\mathbb{I}(u)
as an indicator of the event
u
.
Similarly, for each individual
v
,
let
Y^{v*}(\boldsymbol{\pi})
be the potential outcome
and

C^{v*}(\boldsymbol{\pi})
the potential cost
under
\boldsymbol{\pi}
.
The
potential number of participants under allocation
strategy
\boldsymbol{\pi}
is thus

n^{*}(\boldsymbol{\pi})\triangleq\inf\left\{g\,:\,\sum_{v=1}^{g}C^{v*}(\boldsymbol{\pi})>D\right\}.

We assume that there exists
Q\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\sup_{\boldsymbol{\pi}\in\Pi}n^{*}(\boldsymbol{\pi})\leq Q
almost surely;
in application, this
incurs no loss in generality.
Let
\Delta^{v*}(\boldsymbol{\pi})
be an indicator that
the budget has not been exceeded when individual
v
enters the study under

\boldsymbol{\pi}
.
For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
define the history-value function of
\boldsymbol{\pi}
at
{\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n}
as

V^{n}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n},\boldsymbol{\pi})\triangleq\mathbb{E}\left\{\sum_{v=n}^{Q}\Delta^{v*}(\boldsymbol{\pi})Y^{v*}(\boldsymbol{\pi})\big|{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{n}={\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n}\right\},

where this expectation is over the sampling process.
An optimal
allocation strategy,
\boldsymbol{\pi}^{\mathrm{opt}}
, satisfies

V^{n}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n},\boldsymbol{\pi}^{\mathrm{opt}})\geq V^{n}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n},\boldsymbol{\pi})
for each
n
, all feasible

\boldsymbol{\pi}
, and
{\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n}\in\mathcal{H}^{n}
.

We identify

\boldsymbol{\pi}^{\mathrm{opt}}
in terms of
the data-generating model by making a series of assumptions that are standard for sequential decision problems
(
Tsiatis et al., 2019
)
.
Define the collection of all potential outcomes as

\mathcal{W}\triangleq\left\{{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{v*}(\overline{{\mathbf{a}}}^{v-1}),Y^{v*}(\overline{{\mathbf{a}}}^{v}),C^{v*}(\overline{{\mathbf{a}}}^{v})\,:\,{\mathbf{a}}^{v}\in\psi^{v}\left\{{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{v*}(\overline{{\mathbf{a}}}^{v-1})\right\}\right\}_{v\geq 1}.

Assumption 1

(Strong ignorability)
.

For all
v\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{W}\perp{\mathbf{A}}^{v}|{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{v}
.

Assumption 2

(Consistency)
.

For all
v\in\mathbb{N}
,
{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{v}={\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{v*}(\overline{{\mathbf{A}}}^{v-1})
,
Y^{v}=Y^{v*}(\overline{{\mathbf{A}}}^{v})
,
and
C^{v}=C^{*v}(\overline{{\mathbf{A}}}^{v})
;
i.e., the observed histories, outcomes, and costs are equal to their
counterfactual counterparts under the coupon allocations actually given.

Assumption 3

(Positivity)
.

For all
v\in\mathbb{N}
,
{\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{v}\in\mathcal{H}^{v}
and

{\mathbf{a}}\in\psi^{v}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{v})
, there exists
\epsilon>0
such that

\mathbb{P}({\mathbf{A}}^{v}={\mathbf{a}}|{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{v}={\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{v})\geq\epsilon
.

Under these assumptions, one can express the likelihood for the
counterfactual cumulative outcome under any coupon allocation
strategy in terms of the data-generating model

(
Robins, 2004
)
. However, the curvature of this likelihood may be
zero or near zero in some regions of the parameter space even under
simple network models

(
Crawford et al., 2018
;
Weltz et al., 2024
)
. Consequently, estimators
of parameters indexing the RDS process can be extremely
volatile and online learning based on such
estimators is similarly volatile, especially in small samples. To make online learning tractable, one
must impose additional
structure on the model. This could be done through an informative prior,
though such priors are difficult to posit and have been shown to
exert unacceptably large influence on the operating characteristics of
resulting estimators
(
Weltz et al., 2024
)
. Instead, we posit a simple branching process
working model that is
parsimonious and stable when fit to the RDS data as it accumulates, yet
flexible enough to capture salient features of the RDS process for online
learning.
As noted previously, we do not require this model be correctly specified.

Figure 1
:
RDS is a complex stochastic process that samples without replacement over a social network. The observed RDS sample is composed of coupon exchanges, illustrated by arrows (
\rightarrow
). The unobserved connections between sample participants are represented as dashed lines (
). The observed data resembles a branching process.

2
Reinforcement Learning for RDS

Most
inferential techniques for RDS are based on simplifications
of the RDS process
(
Heckathorn, 1997
;
Rohe, 2015
;
Crawford et al., 2018
)
or graph model

(
Gile and Handcock, 2015
;
Gile, 2011
)
because the network structure is often only weakly
identifiable from the sample
(
Weltz et al., 2024
)
.
Consequently, these estimators can be biased and
unstable. Instead of estimating the graph dynamics directly,
we posit a partially controllable branching process
as a working model for RDS.
This model captures relationships between
recruiters and recruits, and its tractable likelihood
facilitates asymptotic regret guarantees and robust
inference techniques. However, we emphasize that
our inferential approach in Section
3
does not assume the branching
process model is correct.

Let
M^{v}
be the number of recruits of participant
v
,

\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}}^{v}=({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{1}^{v},\ldots,{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{M^{v}}^{v})
the collection
of their covariates,
\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$Y$}}}^{v}=(Y_{1}^{v},\ldots,Y_{M^{v}}^{v})

their response statuses, and
\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$T$}}}^{v}=(T_{1}^{v},\ldots,T_{M^{v}}^{v})

their response times. We write
\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}}^{0},\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$Y$}}}^{0},{\mathbf{A}}^{0}
, and
\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$T$}}}^{0}
to
denote the information collected in the initial sample,
treating the first epoch as recruits of a fictional recruiter
“zero.”
We posit a branching process working model that factors
as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\prod_{j=1}^{J}\prod_{v\in\mathcal{E}_{j}}&f(\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$Y$}}}^{v}|\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}}^{v},\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$T$}}}^{v},M^{v},{\mathbf{A}}^{v},{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{v})f(\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}}^{v}|\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$T$}}}^{v},M^{v},{\mathbf{A}}^{v},{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{v})\times\\
&f(\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$T$}}}^{v}|M^{v},{\mathbf{A}}^{v},{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{v})f(M^{v}|{\mathbf{A}}^{v},{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{v})f({\mathbf{A}}^{v}|{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{v}),\end{split}

(1)

where we define
f({\mathbf{A}}^{0}|{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{0})=f({\mathbf{A}}^{0})
and
assume that
C^{v}
is a known function of

({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{v}
,
{\mathbf{A}}^{v},Y^{v},\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$T$}}}^{v},\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}}^{v},\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$Y$}}}^{v})
.
According to this model, the recruits associated with recruiter

v
are not affected by the coupon allocation to individual
v^{\prime}
(or recruits of
v^{\prime}
) if
v^{\prime}>v
.

During the RDS process, our goal is to guide incentive allocation to maximize the expected
cumulative outcome. This outcome is chosen to characterize study effectiveness, e.g.,
an indicator that a participant consents to disease screening.
Online sequential decision making to optimize a cumulative objective
fits naturally within the framework of reinforcement learning

(
Sutton and Barto, 2018
, RL;)
.
Thompson sampling is an RL algorithm that has been shown to possess
favorable theoretical properties and strong empirical performance on
a wide range of problems
(
Chapelle and Li, 2011
;
Agrawal and Goyal, 2013
;
Gopalan et al., 2014
;
Gopalan and Mannor, 2015
;
Russo and Van Roy, 2016
;
Russo et al., 2018
;
Laber et al., 2018
)
.
However, it has never
been studied as an approach to optimal allocation
in RDS. This context
is especially challenging due to budget constraints and
complex dependence between study participants.

We consider a class of parametric models for the components of the branching process,
specified in Equation (
1
),
which we index by
\boldsymbol{\beta}=\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{y},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{m}\right)\in\mathcal{B}
, where
\mathcal{B}
is a bounded, open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. The contribution to the joint density from the

v
th participant is composed of the following components

\begin{array}[]{ll}f\left(\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$Y$}}}^{v}|\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}}^{v},\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$T$}}}^{v},M^{v},{\mathbf{A}}^{v},{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{v}\right)&=f\left(\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$Y$}}}^{v}|\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}}^{v},\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$T$}}}^{v},M^{v},{\mathbf{A}}^{v},{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{v};\boldsymbol{\beta}_{y}\right),\\
f\left(\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}}^{v}|\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$T$}}}^{v},M^{v},{\mathbf{A}}^{v},{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{v}\right)&=f\left(\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}}^{v}|\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$T$}}}^{v},M^{v},{\mathbf{A}}^{v},{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{v};\boldsymbol{\beta}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}\right),\\
f\left(\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$T$}}}^{v}|M^{v},{\mathbf{A}}^{v},{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{v}\right)&=f\left(\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$T$}}}^{v}|M^{v},{\mathbf{A}}^{v},{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{v};\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}\right),\\
f\left(M^{v}|{\mathbf{A}}^{v},{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{v}\right)&=f\left(M^{v}|\mbox{\boldmath$A$}^{v},{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{v};\boldsymbol{\beta}_{m}\right).\end{array}

(2)

To approximate an optimal strategy after each participant’s arrival, we use the branching process model to simulate potential futures starting from the current RDS sample.
Recall that
Q\in\mathbb{N}
is defined such that
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}n^{*}(\boldsymbol{\pi})\leq Q

almost surely.
For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
{\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n}\in\mathcal{H}^{n}
,
\boldsymbol{\pi}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}
,
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\in\mathcal{B}
,
and
B\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote by

\mathcal{K}^{B}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n},\boldsymbol{\pi};{\boldsymbol{\beta}})
a set of
B
trajectories of length
Q-n

simulated under
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
.
For
b\in B
, the trajectory begins with the RDS information collected so far,
{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}_{b,\boldsymbol{\pi},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}^{n}={\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n}
, and the coupons,

{\mathbf{A}}_{b,\boldsymbol{\pi},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}^{\ell}
, are assigned according to
\pi^{\ell}({\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{\ell}_{b,\boldsymbol{\pi},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}})

for
\ell=n,n+1,\ldots,Q
, i.e.,

\mathcal{K}^{B}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n},\boldsymbol{\pi};{\boldsymbol{\beta}})\triangleq\left\{\left(Y_{b,\boldsymbol{\pi},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}^{n},{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}_{b,\boldsymbol{\pi},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}^{n},{\mathbf{A}}_{b,\boldsymbol{\pi},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}^{n},Y_{b,\boldsymbol{\pi},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}^{n+1},{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}_{b,\boldsymbol{\pi},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}^{n+1},{\mathbf{A}}_{b,\boldsymbol{\pi},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}^{n+1},\ldots,{\mbox{\boldmath$Y$}}_{b,\boldsymbol{\pi},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}^{Q},{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}_{b,\boldsymbol{\pi},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}^{Q}\right)\right\}_{b=1}^{B}.

These simulated
trajectories will be
used to approximate an optimal strategy,

\boldsymbol{\pi}^{\mathrm{opt}}
.
Under Assumptions
1
-
2
, the history-value function reduces to

V^{n}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n},\boldsymbol{\pi};{\boldsymbol{\beta}})=\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\{\sum^{Q}_{v=n}\Delta^{v}(\boldsymbol{\pi})Y^{v}(\boldsymbol{\pi})\big|{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{n}={\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n}\right\}

for any
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\in\mathcal{B}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
history
{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{n}={\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n}
, and
\boldsymbol{\pi}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}
.
Under Assumption
3
, this expectation can be estimated by averaging cumulative reward over the simulated trajectories,

\widehat{V}^{n}_{B}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n},\boldsymbol{\pi};{\boldsymbol{\beta}})=\frac{\sum_{b=1}^{B}\left(\sum_{v=n}^{Q}\Delta^{v}_{b,\boldsymbol{\pi},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}Y^{v}_{b,\boldsymbol{\pi},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\right)}{B}.

The estimated optimal policy under
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
upon observing

{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{n}={\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n}
is thus

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\pi}}^{n}_{B}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n};{\boldsymbol{\beta}})\in\arg\max_{\boldsymbol{\pi}\in\Pi}\widehat{V}^{n}_{B}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n},\boldsymbol{\pi};{\boldsymbol{\beta}})
.

Deciding between allocations that appear to be optimal given
current estimated parameter values and those that might
improve parameter estimates and thereby
lead to better decisions
in the future is a fundamental problem in reinforcement learning. This balance between information gain and optimization
is commonly known as the exploration-exploitation trade-off
in statistics and computer science
(
Berry and Fristedt, 1985
;
Sutton and Barto, 2018
;
Szepesvári, 2010
;
Slivkins et al., 2019
;
Lattimore and Szepesvári, 2020
)
.
We consider a variant of Thompson sampling with clipping
(
Zhang et al., 2020
)
to ensure sufficient exploration when determining coupon allocations.

At each decision point, our method samples a coupon allocation for the newest study participant approximately proportional to the probability that it is optimal.
Let
\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}^{n}
be an estimator
of the parameters indexing the banching process model based on a sample of size
n\in\mathbb{N}

1
1

1

In application, a natural choice for this statistic
(and the one we use for the simulations in Section
4
) is the
maximum likelihood estimator for the parameters indexing the
branching process model; however the theory holds more generally.
, and

\widehat{P}^{n}
denote an estimator of the sampling distribution of
\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}^{n}
.
Given state
{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{n}={\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n}
and
\boldsymbol{\pi}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}
, let

\hat{\rho}^{n}_{B}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n},\boldsymbol{\pi})=\int\mathbb{I}\left\{\boldsymbol{\pi}\in\arg\max_{\boldsymbol{\pi}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\widehat{V}^{n}_{B}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n},\boldsymbol{\pi};{\boldsymbol{\beta}})\right\}d\widehat{P}^{n}({\boldsymbol{\beta}})

be the estimated confidence that

\boldsymbol{\pi}
is the optimal policy.
If
\hat{\rho}^{n}_{B}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n},\boldsymbol{\pi})
is high, it means that
\boldsymbol{\pi}
maximizes the cumulative reward under the points in
\widehat{P}^{n}({\boldsymbol{\beta}})
that are most likely.
Additionally, for
{\mathbf{a}}^{n}\in\psi^{n}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n})
, let

\widehat{\xi}^{n}_{B}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n},{\mathbf{a}}^{n})=\int_{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\mathbb{I}\left\{\pi^{n}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n})={\mathbf{a}}^{n}\right\}d\hat{\rho}^{n}_{B}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n},\boldsymbol{\pi})

be the estimated probability that
{\mathbf{a}}^{n}
is the optimal action.
Thompson sampling with clipping at level
\epsilon\in(0,1)

selects action
{\mathbf{A}}^{n}={\mathbf{a}}^{n}

with probability proportional to
\min\left[1-\epsilon,\max\left\{\epsilon,\widehat{\xi}^{n}_{B}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n},{\mathbf{a}}^{n})\right\}\right]
.
Lemma
11.1
in
the Supplemental Materials
shows that this clipping constraint satisfies the following assumption, which is needed for the theoretical developments in the next section.

Assumption 4

(Bounded action selection probabilities)
.

For all
v\in\mathbb{N}
, assume there exists
\rho_{\min},\rho_{\max}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that
\rho_{\min}\leq\sqrt{1/\mathbb{P}\left({\mathbf{A}}^{v}|{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{v}\right)}\leq\rho_{\max}
with
probability one.

We provide justification for the proposed variant of Thompson Sampling
with the branching process approximation in the following section.

2.1
Asymptotic Regret Bounds

In this section, we show that if the branching model is correctly specified, then
Thompson Sampling with clipping achieves optimal regret. This result is both novel
in its own right and justifies the use of Thompson Sampling for coupon
allocation with our branching
process approximation.

Recall that
\mathcal{B}
is a bounded, open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
We assume a parametric model indexed by fixed but unknown parameter

{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\in\mathcal{B}
.
We construct a weighted maximum likelihood estimator of
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}

by extending the
M-estimation approach described in
Zhang et al. (2021)
to general MDPs.

Zhang et al.
show that the maximum likelihood estimator (MLE) constructed from adaptively sampled data in a linear contextual bandit can converge to a non-normal limit when two arms have the
same mean reward. A normal asymptotic limit
is obtained by re-weighting the likelihood by a function of the propensity score
in such a way that the asymptotic variance
is stabilized
(
Deshpande et al., 2018
;
Hadad et al., 2021
;
Zhan et al., 2023
;
Bibaut and Kallus, 2024
;
Zhan et al., 2024
, see also)
. In RL for RDS
this re-weighting will depend on the coupon assignment distribution.

We consider an asymptotic regime based on complete generations (epochs) of
study participants. For
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}
,
we restrict the information available before the coupon allocation to participant
i
to
\overline{\mathcal{Z}}_{j-1}
.
Additionally, we do not use coupon allocation information associated with individuals in the same epoch as the current study participant.
Define
r(i)
to be the rank of participant
i
’s arrival
time; (i.e., if participant
i
were the third
participant in the study, then
r(i)=3
).
For
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}
,
we assume that the coupon allocation for
participant
i
is based on historical information,
{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}_{i}
, that is an element of sigma field

\sigma\left[\left\{(R^{v},T^{v},{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{v},Y^{v},{\mathbf{A}}^{v},C^{v})\right\}_{v=1}^{r(i)}\cap\left(\overline{\mathcal{Z}}_{j-1}\setminus\{{\mathbf{A}}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}\right)\right].

(3)

Define the complete information associated with the recruits of individual
i
as

{\mbox{\boldmath$D$}}_{i}\triangleq\left\{\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$Y$}}}_{i},\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}}_{i},\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$T$}}}_{i},M_{i},{\mathbf{A}}_{i}\right\}\in\mathscr{D}
,
and
define the field associated with the first
j
generations as

\mathcal{F}_{j}\triangleq\sigma(\overline{\mathcal{Z}}_{j}\setminus\{{\mathbf{A}}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j}})
.
In addition, under the branching process model, we assume that the individuals in
generation
\mathcal{Z}_{j}
are conditionally independent given
\mathcal{F}_{j-1}
and
\{{\mathbf{A}}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}
,
so that

\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{{\mbox{\boldmath$D$}}_{i}\right\}_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}\mid\{{\mathbf{A}}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}},\mathcal{F}_{j-1}\right)=\prod_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}\mathbb{P}\left({\mbox{\boldmath$D$}}_{i}\mid{\mathbf{A}}_{i},\mathcal{F}_{j-1}\right).

Define the “complete generation” likelihood as

\prod_{j=1}^{J}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{Z}_{j}|\mathcal{F}_{j-1}\right)=\prod_{j=1}^{J}\prod_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$Y$}}}_{i},\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}}_{i},\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$T$}}}_{i},M_{i}\right\}|{\mathbf{A}}_{i},\mathcal{F}_{j-1}\right)\mathbb{P}\left({\mathbf{A}}_{i}|{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}_{i}\right),

(4)

where the equality follows from Equation (
3
) under which

\mathbb{P}\left({\mathbf{A}}_{i}|\mathcal{F}_{j-1},\{{\mbox{\boldmath$D$}}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1},k\neq i}\right)=\mathbb{P}\left({\mathbf{A}}_{i}|\mathcal{F}_{j-1}\right)=\mathbb{P}\left({\mathbf{A}}_{i}|{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}_{i}\right)
.

In our context, the estimated optimal policy need not converge to a fixed strategy because of the
non-stationarity of the branching process we use to model RDS. Consequently, as anticipated by
the literature on inference after adaptive sampling, the asymptotic behavior of the
MLE based on (
4
) is difficult to characterize

(
Bibaut and Kallus, 2024
)
. However, we are able to obtain a parametric
rate of convergence after a suitable re-weighting of the likelihood.
Let
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
be the policy followed by the reinforcement algorithm, and
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
be
the policy that samples from among the available coupon allocations with equal probability;
i.e.,
\mathbb{P}_{\widetilde{\pi}}(\boldsymbol{a}|{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}_{i}={\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}_{i})\triangleq 1/\left|\psi_{i}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}_{i})\right|
if

\boldsymbol{a}\in\psi_{i}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}_{i})
and zero otherwise,
and

\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\pi}}(\boldsymbol{a}|{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}_{i}={\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}_{i})\triangleq\mathbb{P}({\mathbf{A}}_{i}=\boldsymbol{a}|{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}_{i}={\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}_{i})
.
We use

W_{i}\triangleq\sqrt{\frac{\mathbb{P}_{\widetilde{\pi}}({\mathbf{A}}_{i}|{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}_{i})}{\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\pi}}({\mathbf{A}}_{i}|{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}_{i})}},

as stabilizing weights
in the log-likelihood.
Our complete generation M-estimator maximizes the weighted log-likelihood

\displaystyle\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{J}\triangleq\arg\max_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\in\mathcal{B}}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}W_{i}l_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}),

\displaystyle\mathrm{where}\ \mathrm{for}\ j\in\mathbb{N}\ \mathrm{and}\ i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1},\ l_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}})\triangleq l({\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\mbox{\boldmath$D$}}_{i})\triangleq\log\left\{\mathbb{P}({\mbox{\boldmath$D$}}_{i}\mid\mathcal{F}_{j-1};{\boldsymbol{\beta}})\right\}.

Below we state the technical assumptions under which we derive regret bounds
for the estimated optimal policy.
We verify that these assumptions hold in the
working model described by Equation (
6
) in Section
2.2
.
Define the number of sample participants in generation
j
as
\kappa_{j}\triangleq|\mathcal{E}_{j}|
and the total number of individuals recruited before generation
j
as
\overline{\kappa}_{j}\triangleq|\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{j-1}|
. Additionally, note that the complete data-generating distribution is specified by the branching process parameter,
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
, and the reinforcement learning policy,
\boldsymbol{\pi}
.
The expectation taken with respect to this distribution is denoted
\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\boldsymbol{\pi}}
.

Assumption 5

(Branching asymptotics)
.

For all recruiters
v\in\mathbb{N}
, and their recruits
j\in\{1,2,\ldots,M^{v}\}
, there exists
\alpha>0
such that
T^{v}_{j}-T^{v}\geq\alpha
with
probability one. In addition, the number of coupons in an allocation is bounded
above by
L_{*}
.
Lastly, the branching process is super-critical; i.e., there exists a random variable
\mathscr{I}
such that for any
\epsilon>0
there exists
\delta>0
such that

\lim_{j\to\infty}\kappa_{j}/m^{j}\to\mathscr{I}\ \ \mathrm{a.s.},\ \mathrm{where}\ \mathbb{P}\left(\mathscr{I}\geq\delta\right)\geq 1-\epsilon.

Assumption 6

(Growing budget asymptotics)
.

The budget grows over time as follows. Let
S^{n}
denote the budget when individual

n
is recruited. For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

0<S^{n}-\sum_{v=1}^{n}C^{v}<C^{*}
with probability one for some
fixed
C^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
.

Assumption 7

(Identifiability and differentiability)
.

The parameter indexing the branching process is identifiable. Additionally, for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}
, the first two derivatives of
l_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}})
with respect to any
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\in\mathcal{B}
exist.

Assumption 8

(Moment conditions)
.

The parameter that indexes the true generative process,
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\in\mathcal{B}
, is in the interior of
\mathcal{B}
. For all
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}
, the first two moments of
l_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})
,
\dot{l}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})
, and
\ddot{l}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})
conditional on
\mathcal{F}_{j-1}
are bounded almost surely.

Assumption 9

(Lipschitz)
.

There exists a real-valued function
g:\mathscr{D}\to\mathbb{R}
such that for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}
, and
{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}
,

|l_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}})-l_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime})|\leq g({\mbox{\boldmath$D$}}_{i})\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}\|_{2},

where
\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\pi}}}\left\{g({\mbox{\boldmath$D$}}_{i})^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{j-1}\right\}
is bounded almost surely.

Assumption 10

(Well-separated maximizer)
.

For any
\epsilon>0
, there exists
J_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\delta>0
such that for all
J\geq J_{0}
,

\inf_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\in\mathcal{B}:\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|_{2}>\epsilon}\left[\frac{1}{\overline{\kappa}_{J}}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}\mathbb{E}_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\pi}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}}\left\{l_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})-l_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}})|\mathcal{F}_{j-1}\right\}\right]\geq\delta\ \ \mathrm{a.s.}

Assumptions
5
and
6
specify the asymptotic regime for the branching process.
Assumption
5
implies that
J\to\infty
and that the generation sizes are consistent with Galton-Watson processes
(
Athreya et al., 2004
)
.
Assumption
6
states that the budget grows in such a way that the
remaining budget is always bounded; this avoids trivial solutions in which maximal
resources are allocated at each time point.
Assumptions
7
-
10
ensure that the log-likelihood is well-behaved.
In Assumptions
7
and
8
, we assume that the log-likelihood is identifiable, two times differentiable, and its components (and their derivatives) have finite second moments.. Assumption
9
limits the complexity of the log-likelihood function so that
the weighted log-likelihood converges uniformly. Assumption
10
requires that
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}
be a “well-seperated” point of maximum and is a standard assumption for consistency; e.g., Theorem 5.7 of
Van der Vaart (2000)
. Note that Assumptions
9
and
10

are unnecessary for consistency if the log-likelihood is concave.

Under Assumption
7
, for any policy
\boldsymbol{\pi}\in\Pi
,
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\in\mathcal{B}
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
, and
i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}
, it follows that

\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\boldsymbol{\pi}}\left\{\dot{l}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}})\mid{\mathbf{A}}_{i},{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}_{i}\right\}=0
.
Consequently,
\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}W_{i}\dot{l}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}})
is a martingale difference with respect to the filtration
\{\mathcal{F}_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\boldsymbol{\pi}}\left\{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}W_{i}\dot{l}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}})\mid\mathcal{F}_{j-1}\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\boldsymbol{\pi}}\left[\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}W_{i}\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\boldsymbol{\pi}}\left\{\dot{l}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}})\mid{\mathbf{A}}_{i},\mathcal{F}_{j-1}\right\}\mid\mathcal{F}_{j-1}\right]=0.

We define the variance of the weighted score function conditioned on this filtration,

\eta_{J}\triangleq\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\pi}}}\left\{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}W_{i}^{2}\dot{l}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})\dot{l}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})^{\top}\mid\mathcal{F}_{j-1}\right\}=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\pi}}}\left\{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}\dot{l}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})\dot{l}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})^{\top}|\mathcal{F}_{j-1}\right\},

where the equality follows from

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\pi}}}\left\{W_{i}^{2}\dot{l}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})\dot{l}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})^{\top}|\mathcal{F}_{j-1}\right\}

\displaystyle=\int W_{i}^{2}\dot{l}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})\dot{l}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})^{\top}f\left({\mbox{\boldmath$D$}}_{i}|{\mathbf{A}}_{i},\mathcal{F}_{j-1};{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\right)\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\pi}}({\mathbf{A}}_{i}|\mathcal{F}_{j-1})d\nu

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\mathbb{P}_{\widetilde{\pi}}({\mathbf{A}}_{i}|{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}_{i})}{\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\pi}}({\mathbf{A}}_{i}|{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}_{i})}\dot{l}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})\dot{l}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})^{\top}f\left({\mbox{\boldmath$D$}}_{i}|{\mathbf{A}}_{i},\mathcal{F}_{j-1};{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\right)\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\pi}}({\mathbf{A}}_{i}|{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}_{i})d\nu

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\pi}}}\left\{\dot{l}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})\dot{l}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})^{\top}|\mathcal{F}_{j-1}\right\}.

We see that re-weighting the outer product
of the score function
removes
the dependence of the estimating equation’s conditional variance on the RL algorithm

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
.

While
\eta_{J}
is a constant in the setting considered by
Zhang et al. (2021)
,
here it is a random variable.
Consequently, characterizing the asymptotic behavior of
\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{J}
requires
new theory for martingale estimating functions constructed from controllable branching
processes.
For matrix
A
, let
\sigma_{\min}(A)
be its minimum eigenvalue.
In addition, let
I_{k}
be the
k
dimensional identity matrix.
To extend asymptotic theory for
M
-estimators collected under a contextual bandit
to the more general setting of an MDP, we make use of the following standard assumptions.

Assumption 11

(Martingale stabilizing variance)
.

As
J\to\infty
, there exists a sequence of constant (i.e., not random)
positive definite matrices

\{\Sigma_{J}\}_{J\geq 1}
such that

\Sigma_{J}^{-1/2}{\eta_{J}}\Sigma_{J}^{-1/2}\overset{p}{\to}U,

where
U
is a (random) positive
definite matrix and
U\succ 0
with probability one.

Assumption 12

(Information accumulation)
.

For some
\delta>0
,
\lim\inf_{J\rightarrow\infty}\sigma_{\min}\left\{\eta_{J}/\overline{\kappa}_{J}\right\}\geq\delta

with probability one.

Assumption 13

(Equicontinuity)
.

There exists an
\epsilon_{\ddot{l}}>0
and a function
f:\mathscr{D}\to\mathbb{R}
such that for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}
and
0<\epsilon\leq\epsilon_{\ddot{l}}
, there exists
\delta_{\epsilon}
such that

\sup_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\in\mathcal{B}:\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|_{2}\leq\delta_{\epsilon}}\|\ddot{l}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}})-\ddot{l}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})\|_{2}\leq\epsilon f({\mbox{\boldmath$D$}}_{i})\ \ \mathrm{a.s.},

and
\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\pi}}}\left\{f({\mbox{\boldmath$D$}}_{i})|\mathcal{F}_{j-1}\right\}
is bounded almost surely.

Assumption
11
requires that the conditional variance of the log-likelihood components converges. This assumption is the crux of our extention from the contextual bandit framework of
Zhang et al. (2021)
to more general MDPs. It is also standard in asymptotic theory
for martingales, e.g., Theorem 3.2 in
Hall and Heyde (2014)
.
Assumption
12
requires that information accumulates over time.
Assumption
13
ensures the equicontinuity of the empirical Fisher information.

Theorem 2.1
.

For
\delta>0
, define the event
E_{\mathscr{I}}=\left\{\mathscr{I}>\delta\right\}
, where
\mathscr{I}
is defined in Assumption
5
.
Under Assumptions
1
-
13
,
as
J\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{J}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}=O_{p}(1/\sqrt{\overline{\kappa}_{J}})

(5)

on event
E_{\mathscr{I}}
.

We consider the cumulative reward of policies implemented over study participants as they arrive at the study, represented by data
\mathcal{D}^{\kappa}\triangleq\left\{(R^{v},T^{v},{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{v},Y^{v},{\mathbf{A}}^{v},C^{v})\right\}_{v=1}^{\kappa}
for
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
. Consequently, we require a Lipchitz condition for the log-likelihood of this arrival process to establish asymptotic regret bounds. For
v\in\mathbb{N}
, define the log-likelihood components of the arrival process as

\displaystyle q^{v}({\boldsymbol{\beta}})\triangleq q\left\{{\boldsymbol{\beta}},(R^{v},T^{v},{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{v},Y^{v},{\mathbf{A}}^{v})\right\}\triangleq\log\left[\mathbb{P}\left\{(R^{v},T^{v},{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{v},Y^{v},{\mathbf{A}}^{v})|{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}^{v-1},{\mathbf{A}}^{v-1},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right\}\right].

Assumption 14

(Lipschitz 2)
.

There exists a real-valued function
e
such that for all
v\in\mathbb{N}
, and
{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}
,

|q^{v}({\boldsymbol{\beta}})-q^{v}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime})|\leq e\left\{(R^{v},T^{v},{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{v},Y^{v},{\mathbf{A}}^{v})\right\}\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}\|_{2},

where for any
\boldsymbol{\pi}\in\Pi
,
\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*},\boldsymbol{\pi}}\left[e\left\{(R^{v},T^{v},{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{v},Y^{v},{\mathbf{A}}^{v})\right\}^{2}\right]
is bounded.

Define
t_{\min}\geq 0
as the minimum processing time for an individual and
t_{\max}>0
as the coupon expiration time so that
t_{\min}\leq T_{j}^{v}-T^{v}\leq t_{\max}
for any
v\in\mathbb{N}
and
j\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,M^{v}\right\}
. For a sample of size
\kappa
, define
J_{\kappa}
as the last complete epoch induced by the expiration of coupons,
J_{\kappa}\triangleq\max\{j:\forall i\in\mathcal{E}_{j},\ T_{i}+t_{\max}<T^{\kappa}\}
.
Additionally, define
n_{J}\triangleq\min\left\{\kappa:J_{\kappa}=J\right\}
as the index of the first individual recruited after all the coupons associated with members of epoch
J
have expired.

Theorem 2.2
.

For any
{\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n_{J}}\in\mathcal{H}^{n_{J}}
, define

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{J}\in\arg\max_{\boldsymbol{\pi}\in\Pi}V^{n_{J}}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n_{J}},\boldsymbol{\pi};\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{J})
. For
\delta>0
, define the event
E_{\mathscr{I}}=\left\{\mathscr{I}>\delta\right\}
, where
\mathscr{I}
is defined in Assumption
5
.
Under Assumptions
1
-
14
and for a fixed
{\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n_{J}}\in\mathcal{H}^{n_{J}}
, it follows that

V^{n_{J}}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n_{J}},\boldsymbol{\pi}^{\mathrm{opt}};{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})-V^{n_{J}}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n_{J}},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{J};{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})=O_{p}\left(1/\sqrt{\overline{\kappa}_{J}}\right)

as
J\to\infty
on event
E_{\mathscr{I}}
.

The proofs of Theorems
2.1
and
2.2
are in
the Supplemental Materials. Lemma
10.1
in the Supplementary Materials ensures that
\lim_{\kappa\to\infty}J_{\kappa}\to\infty
a.s. under Assumption
5
, implying that inference based on the last complete epoch will achieve the asymptotic guaranties of Theorems
2.1
and
2.2
. Note that
\lim_{\kappa\to\infty}J_{\kappa}\to\infty
a.s. implies
n_{J}<\infty
for all
J\in\mathbb{N}
.

2.2
A Branching Process Example

In this section, we provide an example of a working branching process model that might
be used in the context of RL-RDS and illustrate how the assumptions used in
Theorems (
2.1
) and (
2.2
) can be verified for this
model.
Define
T_{i,l}
,
{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i,l}
,
Y_{i,l}
, and
A_{i,l}
for
l=1,\ldots,M_{i}
as the arrival times, covariates, rewards, and coupon types associated with the potential recruits of recruiter
i
respectively.
Define
\mathbb{A}=\{-1,1\}
as the set of possible coupon types (these might reflect different calls to
action for example), and
{\mbox{\boldmath$Z$}}_{i,l}=\left(1,{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i,l},{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i,l}{\mathbb{I}}\left(A_{i,l}=-1\right)\right)
.

We consider a working model of the form:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(M_{i}=m_{i}|{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}_{i},{\mathbf{A}}_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda^{m_{i}}/m_{i}!}{\sum_{\ell=0}^{|\mathbf{A}_{i}|}(\lambda^{\ell}/\ell!)},m_{i}=0,\ldots,|\mathbf{A}_{i}|,

\displaystyle T_{i,l}-T_{i}|{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}_{i},{\mathbf{A}}_{i},M_{i}

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\mathrm{Truncated\ Exponential}(\zeta,t_{\min},t_{\max}),\,l=1,\ldots,M_{i},

\displaystyle{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i,l}|{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}_{i},U_{i,l},{\mathbf{A}}_{i},M_{i}

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\mathrm{Normal}\left(\boldsymbol{\phi}_{a}+G_{a}{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i},\Sigma_{a}\right),\,l=1,\ldots,M_{i},a=A_{i,l},

\displaystyle Y_{i,l}|{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}_{i},{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i},U_{i,l},{\mathbf{A}}_{i},M_{i}

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\mathrm{Bernoulli}\left\{\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(-{{\mbox{\boldmath$Z$}}_{i,l}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y}\right)}\right\},\,l=1,\ldots,M_{i},

(6)

where
\lambda,\zeta\in\mathbb{R}
,
\{G_{a}\}_{a\in\mathbb{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
,

\{\boldsymbol{\phi}_{a}\}_{a\in\mathbb{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times 1}
,
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
,
and
\{\Sigma_{a}\}_{a\in\mathbb{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
.
In the context of a randomized experiment,
Assumption 1 (strong ignorability), Assumption 3 (positivity),
and Assumption 6 (budget growth) can be ensured to hold by design. Thus, we assume them
as a matter of course. Assumption 2 (consistency) is also assumed (this is standard
as consistency is sometimes considered as an axiom rather than an assumption).
We evaluate the assumptions that are sufficient for the convergence and regret results to hold for our working model in the Supplementary Materials. We verify them under the model conditions:

(C1)

the same number of coupons, defined as
L\in\mathbb{N}
, are given to each participant,
t_{\min}>0
, and

\lambda
is such that

\lambda\in\left\{\lambda:\sum_{m_{i}=1}^{L}m_{i}\frac{\lambda^{m_{i}}/m_{i}!}{\sum_{\ell=0}^{L}(\lambda^{\ell}/\ell!)}>1\right\};

(C2)

for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
A_{i,l}
is constant across
l\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,M_{i}\right\}
;

(C3)

the set of coupon allocations available for each participant is constant throughout the study;

(C4)

\mathcal{X}
is compact;

(C5)

\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{q}
is convex and compact, and
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\in\mathcal{B}
is an interior point of
\mathcal{B}
;

(C6)

\frac{1}{|\mathbb{A}|}\sum_{a\in\mathbb{A}}\log\|G_{a}\|_{2}<0,

recalling that
\|\cdot\|
is the spectral norm.

Under Conditions (C1)-(C6), we show that there exists a random variable
\mathscr{I}
s.t.

\mathscr{I}=\lim_{j\to\infty}m^{-j}\kappa_{j}\quad\mathrm{a.s.},\quad\mathbb{P}\left(\mathscr{I}\geq\delta\right)\geq\epsilon.

(7)

where
\mathbb{E}\left(\mathscr{I}\right)=1

(
Athreya et al., 2004
)
. Consequently, event
E_{\mathscr{I}}
is well defined. Condition (C6) ensures that the auto-regressive covariate process is not explosive.

Theorem 2.3
.

Assume that Conditions (C1)-(C6) hold as well as Assumptions 1-3 and 6. Then, under the
working model given above, the conclusions of Theorems (
2.1
) and
(
2.2
) hold.

The proof of the preceding result involves deriving a new weak law of large numbers for Galton-Watson branching processes to verify Assumption
11
.
Theorem
2.3
implies that for the posited model, policy-search with Thompson sampling attains favorable regret bounds as
J\to\infty
. Because Equation
7
and Condition (C1) satisfy Assumption
5
, we know that Lemma
10.1
is also satisfied under the assumptions made in Theorem
2.3
.
In the next section, we discuss inference
given an RL-RDS sample without assuming an underlying branching model.

3
Inference for RL-RDS

In this section, we derive valid inference for functionals of the population network model.
To account for the underlying social network and the idiosyncrasies of RDS,
we do not assume that data-generating model is a branching process. Instead, we
consider a dynamic network model indexed by
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}\in{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
for which
we derive asymptotic confidence sets. Projections of these
regions are then used to conduct inference for functionals of the data-generating
model, e.g., disease prevalence, rate of risky behavior,
attitudes toward public health services, etc.

We recall that the data-generating model (i.e.,
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}
) need not be identifiable
under an RDS sampling scheme. Nevertheless, it is still possible to obtain
valid confidence intervals by inverting a test

(
Robins, 2004
;
Laber and Murphy, 2011
, e.g., see)
. Our test is based on the
likelihood ratio for the covariate distribution in the branching process working model.
We reiterate that this is only used to construct a test and that we are not assuming
that this model is correct.
Let
\ell_{\kappa}^{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\left({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\right)
be the log-likelihood
2
2

2

Note that in this inference procedure, we use the complete sample
v\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,\kappa\right\}
because we are no longer restricted to epoch structured data by the branching process.
of the branching process covariate model for a collection of
\kappa
subjects sampled under the
RDS process when
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
is the true parameter.
Define the MLE of the working model at
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
as

\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}^{\kappa}({\boldsymbol{\theta}})\in\arg\max_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\in\mathcal{B}}\ell_{\kappa}^{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\left({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\right).

At the true parameter,
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}
, we let
\ell_{\kappa}\triangleq\ell_{\kappa}^{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}
and
\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}^{\kappa}\triangleq\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}^{\kappa}({\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*})
.

Recall that
\mathcal{B}
is a bounded, open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Let
s:{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\to\mathcal{B}
be a fixed function. Our confidence region is based on the
distribution of the proximity of
\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}^{\kappa}({\boldsymbol{\theta}})
to
s({\boldsymbol{\theta}})
. In
our application, we choose
s({\boldsymbol{\theta}})
to be an asymptotic limit of

\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}^{\kappa}({\boldsymbol{\theta}})
though other choices are possible and may be more
appropriate in other contexts; hence, we let
s
be arbitrary.
Given
s
, define the sampling distribution of the log-likelihood ratio statistic
at
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
as

-2\left[\ell_{\kappa}^{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\left\{s({\boldsymbol{\theta}})\right\}-\ell_{\kappa}^{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\left\{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}^{\kappa}({\boldsymbol{\theta}})\right\}\right]\sim P_{\kappa}^{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\left(s\right),

and the
1-\alpha
quantile of
P_{\kappa}^{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\left(s\right)
as

{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1-\alpha,\kappa}^{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(s)
.
A confidence region for
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}
is

\Gamma_{1-\alpha,\kappa}(s)=\left\{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}:-2\left[\ell_{\kappa}\left\{s({\boldsymbol{\theta}})\right\}-\ell_{\kappa}\left\{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}^{\kappa}\right\}\right]\leq{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1-\alpha,\kappa}^{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\left(s\right)\right\}.

(8)

For
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in\Theta
, we sample from
P_{\kappa}^{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\left(s\right)
by simulating a dataset of size
\kappa
under RDS at
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
and calculate
-2\left[\ell_{\kappa}^{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\left\{s({\boldsymbol{\theta}})\right\}-\ell_{\kappa}^{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\left\{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}^{\kappa}({\boldsymbol{\theta}})\right\}\right]
given this data. We can approximate
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1-\alpha,\kappa}^{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(s)
to arbitrary precision by generating a large number of draws from
P_{\kappa}^{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\left(s\right)
.
That
\Gamma_{1-\alpha,\kappa}(s)
achieves nominal coverage is easily verified as, by
construction, we have

\mathbb{P}\left\{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}\in\Gamma_{1-\alpha,\kappa}(s)\right\}=\mathbb{P}\left\{-2\left[\ell_{\kappa}\left\{s({\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*})\right\}-\ell_{\kappa}\left\{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}^{\kappa}\right\}\right]\leq{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1-\alpha,\kappa}^{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\left(s\right)\right\}=1-\alpha.

This equality holds in finite samples regardless of the function
s
.

The preceding result shows that
\Gamma_{1-\alpha,\kappa}(s)
achieves nominal coverage, we now
consider another key attribute of this interval, its asymptotic concentration.
To do this, we make the following additional
assumptions.
First, we assume that the average log-likelihood for each
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\in\mathcal{B}
converges to a finite limit.

Assumption 15

(Pointwise convergence)
.

For any
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
and for each
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\in\mathcal{B}
,

\ell_{\kappa}^{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}})/\kappa
converges almost surely to a finite
limit
\overline{\ell}^{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}})
as
\kappa\rightarrow\infty
.
Define

\bar{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}({\boldsymbol{\theta}})=\arg\max_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\in\mathcal{B}}\overline{\ell}^{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}})
, then
\bar{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}({\boldsymbol{\theta}})
is finite almost surely.

Under this assumption, the asymptotic limit of
\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}^{\kappa}({\boldsymbol{\theta}})
is well-defined. We
suggest using this limit,
\overline{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}({\boldsymbol{\theta}})
, as the function
s({\boldsymbol{\theta}})
. Let
\Gamma_{1-\alpha,\kappa}
denote the confidence set for
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}
under this
choice.
We also assume that the average log-likelihood over a compact set stays strictly concave asymptotically.

Assumption 16
.

For any
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in\Theta
, the log-likelihood of the working model,
{\ell}^{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}_{\kappa}
, is concave for every
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
. Additionally,
\dot{\ell}_{{\kappa}}^{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\left({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\right)/{\kappa}=O_{p}(1)
as
\kappa\to\infty
. Lastly, for any
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in\Theta
, and any compact set
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{B}
, there exists
\delta_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}>0
such that

\lim_{\kappa\to\infty}\sup_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}\sigma_{\min}\left\{-\ddot{\ell}^{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}_{\kappa}\left({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\right)/\kappa\right\}\geq\delta_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\ \ \mathrm{a.s.}

Define the equivalence class

{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{*}=\left\{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}:\overline{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}({\boldsymbol{\theta}})=\overline{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}({\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*})\right\}

as the set of network models that have
the same asymptotic branching process parameter as the true network model. If there is a unique
\bar{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}({\boldsymbol{\theta}})
for each
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
, then
{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{*}=\left\{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}\right\}
. Otherwise, the size of
{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{*}
can be thought of as the “price” our inference approach pays for using a working model to perform inference on
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}
instead of the true data-generating model. In Theorem
3.1
, we show that Assumptions
15
-
16
with Conditions (C2) and (C4) imply that
\Gamma_{1-\alpha,\kappa}
concentrates around
{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{*}
as
\kappa\to\infty
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Under Assumptions
1
-
3
,
15
-
16
,

\bar{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}({\boldsymbol{\theta}})
is unique and

\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}^{\kappa}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}({\boldsymbol{\theta}})\overset{p}{\to}\bar{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}({\boldsymbol{\theta}})

as
\kappa\to\infty
. Additionally, for any
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\notin{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{*}
,

\lim_{\kappa\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left({\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in\Gamma_{1-\alpha,\kappa}\right)\to 0.

The proof of Theorem
3.1
is in the Supplemental Materials.
If we use the branching process in Equation
6
as the working model, we can substitute Assumption
17
, Condition (2), and Condition (4) for Assumption
16
in Theorem
3.1
.

Assumption 17
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define fields
\mathcal{F}^{n}=\sigma\left[\left\{R^{v},T^{v},{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{v},Y^{v},{\mathbf{A}}^{v},C^{v}\right\}_{v=1}^{n}\right]
.
For any
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in\Theta
, there exists a positive definite
\Delta
,
\delta>0
, and
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\forall{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}
and
n\geq N
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\left\{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{R^{n}}{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{R^{n}}{\mathbb{I}}({\mathbf{A}}^{R^{n}}={\mathbf{a}})\mid\mathcal{F}^{n-1}\right\}\succeq\Delta,\quad\mathbb{P}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\left({\mathbf{A}}^{R^{n}}={\mathbf{a}}\mid\mathcal{F}^{n-1}\right)\geq\delta.

We note that setting
s({\boldsymbol{\theta}})=\bar{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}({\boldsymbol{\theta}})
necessitates approximating
\bar{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}({\boldsymbol{\theta}})
for an arbitrary
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
. Algorithm
1
in
the Supplemental Materials
describes our method of approximation as well as the full procedure for constructing
\Gamma_{1-\alpha,\kappa}
when
s({\boldsymbol{\theta}})=\bar{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}({\boldsymbol{\theta}})
.

4
RL-RDS Simulations

We conducted a series of simulation experiments to evaluate the operating characteristics
of RL-RDS. To allow comparisons with the two-stage procedure proposed by

McFall et al. (2021)
(see also
VanOrsdale (2023)
),
we consider the setting in which the goal is to recruit the largest subset of
people in a hidden population with a given binary trait, e.g., undiagnosed HIV.
The outcome is thus an indicator of this trait.
We estimate the optimal policy,
\boldsymbol{\pi}^{\mathrm{opt}}
, using RL-RDS. After collecting the sample, we
use the confidence set derived in Section
3
to construct projection intervals
for parameters indexing the target population’s network and covariate models.

The hidden population network is generated as follows. Given population size
N
, each population member
v\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}
is assigned attributes
{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{v}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}\mathrm{Normal}({\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}},\Sigma)
, where

{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, and
\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
.
Define
\mathscr{A}_{N}\in\{0,1\}^{N\times N}
to be the adjacency matrix representing links between the population members.
We construct the adjacency matrix using a latent distance network model, which is known to be flexible and projective
(
Spencer and Shalizi, 2017
)
.
This model specifies the probability of a connection between individuals
i
and
j
as

\mathbb{P}\left\{\left(\mathscr{A}_{N}\right)_{i,j}=1\big|{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{i}={\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}^{i},{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{j}={\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}^{j}\right\}=1/\left[1+\exp\left\{-\left(\rho_{0}-\rho_{1}\|{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}^{i}-{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}^{j}\|_{2}\right)\right\}\right],

where
\rho_{0},\rho_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
.

To characterize the evolution of RDS, for each
v\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}
, define

\mathcal{N}^{v}=\{j\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}\setminus\{v\}:\left(\mathscr{A}_{N}\right)_{v,j}=1\}

as the neighborhood of
v
.
Label the set of individual
v
’s potential recruits
as
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{v}
; i.e., the un-recruited members of
\mathcal{N}^{v}
when
v
is recruited. Furthermore, define
\mathcal{M}^{v}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{M}^{v}_{1}
as the coupon-constrained set of potential recruits for participant
v
.
If
|\mathcal{M}_{1}^{v}|\leq|{\mathbf{A}}^{v}|
, then
\mathcal{M}_{2}^{v}\equiv\mathcal{M}_{1}^{v}
. If
|\mathcal{M}_{1}^{v}|>|{\mathbf{A}}^{v}|
, then sample
|{\mathbf{A}}^{v}|
recruits from
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{v}
according to probabilities
\mathbf{p}^{v}=u({\mathbf{A}}^{v},\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}}^{v})
, where
u:\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{X}^{|\mathcal{M}_{1}^{v}|}\to[0,1]^{|\mathcal{M}_{1}^{v}|}
, and label them
\mathcal{M}_{2}^{v}
.
This allows for the neighbor selection process to depend on the characteristics of the neighbors and the coupon allocation type.
When individual
v
is recruited, we assign arrival times,
T^{v}_{j}
, to the edges between recruiter,
v
, and hidden population members,
j\in\mathcal{M}_{2}^{v}
, such that
T^{v}_{j}-T^{v}\sim\mathrm{Truncated\ Exponential}(\zeta,t_{\min},t_{\max})
. At time
T^{v}_{j}
, participant
j
enters the study (if they have not been previously recruited) and edge
\{v,j\}
is recorded.
The reward is independently drawn for recruit
j
of pariticpant
v
according to
Y^{v}_{j}|{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{v}_{j},A^{v}_{j}\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}\left[1/\left\{1+\exp\left(-{{\mbox{\boldmath$Z$}}^{v}_{j}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y}\right)\right\}\right]
.
We note that the complete data-generating process is parameterized by
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\triangleq(\rho_{0},\rho_{1},{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}},\Sigma,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y},\zeta,u,t_{\min},t_{\max})
.

4.1
Policies

We evaluate the performance of RL-RDS against a suite of alternative strategies. At each step, the researcher can choose from a finite selection of coupon types.
The fixed allocation policies (i.e., those that give the same coupon allocation type to all participants) represent the current standard in RDS. The train-and-implement policy (aka, explore-than-exploit) mimics the procedure used by
McFall et al. (2021)
, which determines an incentive strategy using a pilot study.
We describe each strategy below.

1.

Fixed
offers a fixed coupon allocation
{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}
to every study participant. If
{\mathbf{a}}\notin\psi^{v}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{v})
, then pick a random coupon allocation from
\psi^{v}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{v})
to give to the
v^{\mathrm{th}}
study participant.

2.

Random
offers a random element of
\psi^{v}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{v})
to the
v^{\mathrm{th}}
study participant.

3.

Train-and-Implement
uses half of the budget for a “pilot study,” in which the the Random policy is used to assign coupon allocations. It then conducts policy search using the pilot study data to estimate the branching process working model. This estimated policy (without updating) is then used to determine coupon allocations for the remainder of the budget.

4.

RL-RDS
uses the Random policy to assign coupon allocations to participants in a short “warm-up” period (
50
participants in the simulations below). Then, it performs policy search with Thompson sampling
for the remainder of the budget.

To conduct RL-RDS, we use the following space of policies,
\Pi
. Define
\alpha_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, and
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}=(\alpha_{0},\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}_{1})
. For
n\in\mathbb{N}
and state
{\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n}\in\mathcal{H}^{n}
, we consider policies of the form
\boldsymbol{\pi}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n})=\left\{\pi^{n}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n}),\pi^{n+1}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n+1}),\cdots\right\}
such that for
v\geq n
,

\pi^{v}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{v})=\pi^{v}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{v},\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$})=\pi^{v}({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}^{v},\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$})=g^{v}\left[\frac{1}{1+\exp\left\{-(\alpha_{0}+{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}^{v}}^{\top}\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}_{1})\right\}}\right],

(9)

where
g^{v}:(0,1)\to\phi^{v}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{v})
maps a continuous score (dependent on the participant’s covariates) to a coupon allocation,
{\mathbf{a}}^{v}\in\psi^{v}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{v})
.
We first draw
\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}^{n}
from the
generalized bootstrap estimator of the
sampling distribution of
the MLE
(
Chatterjee and Bose, 2005
)
. We then generate synthetic data sets,

\mathcal{K}^{B}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n},\boldsymbol{\pi};{\boldsymbol{\beta}})\triangleq\left\{\left(Y_{b,\boldsymbol{\pi},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}^{n},{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}_{b,\boldsymbol{\pi},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}^{n},{\mathbf{A}}_{b,\boldsymbol{\pi},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}^{n},Y_{b,\boldsymbol{\pi},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}^{n+1},{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}_{b,\boldsymbol{\pi},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}^{n+1},{\mathbf{A}}_{b,\boldsymbol{\pi},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}^{n+1},\ldots,{\mbox{\boldmath$Y$}}_{b,\boldsymbol{\pi},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}^{Q},{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}_{b,\boldsymbol{\pi},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}^{Q}\right)\right\}_{b=1}^{B},

and calculate

\widehat{V}^{n}_{B}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n},\boldsymbol{\pi};{\boldsymbol{\beta}})=\frac{\sum_{b=1}^{B}\left(\sum_{v=n}^{Q}\Delta^{v}_{b,\boldsymbol{\pi},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}Y^{v}_{b,\boldsymbol{\pi},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\right)}{B}.

for each
\boldsymbol{\pi}\in\Pi
. To determine the coupon allocation for the current study participant, we set
\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}_{B}^{n}=\arg\max_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\widehat{V}^{n}_{B}({\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}^{n},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}};\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}^{n})
,
and assign
{\mathbf{a}}^{n}=\pi^{n}({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}^{n},\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}_{B}^{n})
. Alternatively, we could use a grid approximation to
\Pi
or a gradient descent method to approximate
\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}_{B}^{n}
.

4.2
Results

In the following simulations, we set the hidden population size to
N=5,000
. The recruitment process begins with an initial sample of
25
individuals randomly drawn from the population,
\left|\mathcal{E}_{0}\right|=25
. The graph model is defined by
\rho^{*}_{0}=0
,
{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}^{*}=(1,1,1)
, and
\Sigma^{*}=\mathrm{diag}\left\{(10,10,10)\right\}
. We vary
\rho^{*}_{1}\in\{1,2\}
, to compare simulation experiments in dense and sparse network settings respectively.
The researchers have access to three types of coupon allocations
\mathcal{A}\triangleq\{{\mathbf{a}}_{1},{\mathbf{a}}_{2},{\mathbf{a}}_{3}\}
that correspond to three coupon types
\mathbb{A}\triangleq\{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\}
. Each allocation has
5
coupons. We found that limiting the number of coupons given to each study participant in the pilot study and the warm-up period of the T&I and the RLRDS policies respectively allows us to observe the effects of the learned policies earlier in the sampling process. Consequently, we give two coupons to individuals in the pilot and warm-up period, and increase the allotment to
5
coupons afterwards. Additionally,
C^{v}\equiv 1
for all
v\in\mathbb{N}
. For recruit
j
of participant
v
, define the reward model components as
{\mbox{\boldmath$Z$}}_{j}^{v}\triangleq\left\{1,{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{v}_{j},\mathbb{I}(A^{v}_{j}=a_{2}){\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{v}_{j},\mathbb{I}(A^{v}_{j}=a_{3}){\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{v}_{j}\right\}
and
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y}^{*}\triangleq(-1,3{\mbox{\boldmath$k$}},-3{\mbox{\boldmath$k$}},-6{\mbox{\boldmath$k$}})
, where
{\mbox{\boldmath$k$}}\triangleq(1,-1,-1)
. Furthermore, we define the neighbor selection probability distribution as
\mathbf{p}^{v}=u({\mathbf{A}}^{v},\underline{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}}^{v})
such that

\displaystyle p^{v}_{j}=\begin{cases}&\frac{\left\|{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{v}-{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{v}_{j}\right\|_{2}^{2}}{\sum_{j\in\mathcal{M}_{1}^{v}}\left\|{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{v}-{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{v}_{j}\right\|_{2}^{2}},\ \mathrm{if}\ {\mathbf{A}}^{v}={\mathbf{a}}_{2},\\
&1/|\mathcal{M}_{1}^{v}|,\ \mathrm{otherwise,}\end{cases}

where
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{v}
is the set of participant
v
’s potential recruits (as defined in the previous section). This implies that recruiters with the second coupon type will be more likely to recruit neighbors that are different from them.
The basic policy objective is clear: we want to ensure that coupon type 1,
{\mathbf{a}}_{1}
, is used to recruit individuals with covariates that satisfy
{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{v}}^{\top}{\mbox{\boldmath$k$}}>0
, and coupon type 3,
{\mathbf{a}}_{3}
, is used to recruit individuals with covariates that satisfy
{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{v}}^{\top}{\mbox{\boldmath$k$}}<0
.
We also wish to recruit individuals with large
|{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{v}}^{\top}{\mbox{\boldmath$k$}}|
because this will increase the probability of the desired outcome contingent on the correct coupon type being awarded.
Consequently, it is possible that coupon type 2,
{\mathbf{a}}_{2}
, is optimal for participant
v
if
|{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{v}}^{\top}{\mbox{\boldmath$k$}}|
is low because it increases the likelihood of observing high values in the future.
We define the policy space by specifying the function
g^{v}
from (
9
) as

g^{v}(z)=\begin{cases}{\mathbf{a}}_{1},&\mathrm{if}\ z>0.66,\\
{\mathbf{a}}_{2},&\mathrm{if}\ 0.66>z\geq 0.33,\\
{\mathbf{a}}_{3},&\mathrm{if}\ 0.33>z.\end{cases}

This policy space implies that correctly assigning coupons
1
or
3
will depend on the sign of the
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
components. The frequency of coupon 2 allocation will be determined by the magnitude of
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
. This structure makes finding an optimal policy computationally feasible while maintaining sufficient difficulty to showcase the strength of RL-RDS. Lastly, we set
\zeta=1
,
t_{\min}=0
, and
t_{\max}=3
.

Figure
2
illustrates the estimated value of policies in both the sparse and dense network settings. It indicates that RL-RDS outperforms all competitor policies by a significant margin in each regime.
In the Supplemental Materials,
we test RL-RDS under two additional simulation paradigms. We vary the value/cost and number of coupons given to RDS participants in these experiments. In these contexts, we introduce another branching process working model that incorporates the incentive value in the covariate and reward models (while reducing their dimensionality). This model is described by Equation
36
in the Supplemental Materials.
Figures
4
and
5
confirm that RL-RDS outperforms all competitor policies by a significant margin under multiple graph density and coupon allocation settings.

We construct 95% confidence regions for the full network model,
(\rho_{0}^{*},\rho_{1}^{*},{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}^{*},\Sigma^{*})
, with a variety of inference procedures.
In these experiments, we assume that
\Sigma^{*}
is diagonal and
q
is known to reduce the computational burden of the inference methods.
We also add an additional network setting,
\rho_{1}^{*}=0.5
(corresponding to a higher density network).
Table
1
depicts coverage results for these confidence regions.
The table indicates that our simulation-based inference (SBI) technique, which is described by Algorithm
1
in the Supplemental Materials,
achieves nominal coverage or greater across all graph settings.
In the sparse setting,
bootstrapping with the log-likelihood ratio (BS LLR) and approximate bayesian computation (ABC) achieve nominal coverage as well. However, as the the graph becomes denser, ABC and BS LLR’s coverage decreases while SBI’s coverage stays above 95%; i.e. the comparison methods fail to adequately quantify uncertainty while our method succeeds. The details of ABC, BS LLR, and bootstrapping with the wald interval (BS WI) are described in
the Supplemental Materials.

Table
2
reports 95% projection interval coverage results for each dimension of the average covariate value; i.e.,
\nu_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
.
We see that the projection intervals associated with every inference method have high coverage except for BS WI. This is unsurprising since we conduct inference calibrated to have 95% coverage over all network parameters simultaneously. Encouragingly, our method, SBI, has a smaller interval length than contenders that achieve nominal coverage (BS LLR and ABC).

5
Discussion

We showed that RL-driven adaptive RDS can lead to
dramatic
improvements in the effectiveness of RDS. In the
course of deriving an RL strategy, we: (i) showed that
a branching process approximation to RDS is useful for
guiding coupon selection without having to fully model
the underlying system dynamics; (ii) extended
asymptotic theory for adaptively sampled M-estimators
to Markov Decision Processes; and (iii) developed novel
methods for post adaptive-RDS inference that remain
valid even when the true model is not identifiable.

This work is the first to consider fully adaptive
RDS methods using RL. Consequently,
there are a number of open problems and interesting
directions for future work. One such direction
is the development of a regret
bound that explicitly depends on the quality of the
branching process approximation. While branching
processes
have been used extensively to model epidemics
evolving on networks, the quality of these approximations
remains an important open question. Another direction
for future work regards statistical efficiency. As
noted
in the introduction, it is possible to fold measures
of information gain into participant outcomes to improve
power in post-study analyses. However,
how best to do this for various network models is not clear.
Finally, as we considered projective dynamic graph models,
exploring whether there is a more
general class of models to which the asymptotic theory
still applies is yet another potential area for future research.

Inference Algorithms

Algorithm
1
generates an approximation of the confidence interval described in Section
3
.
Recall that we need to approximate
\bar{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}({\boldsymbol{\theta}})
for an arbitrary
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in\Theta
. We do this by first generating a large network for
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
and simulating an RDS study of size
K>>\kappa
over this network. Using this simulated sample, we maximize the log-likelihood of the branching process working model to obtain
\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}^{K}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}({\boldsymbol{\theta}})
.

for

{\boldsymbol{\theta}}

B

\kappa

{\boldsymbol{\theta}}

8
Proof of Theorem
2.1

8.1
Supporting Martingale Limit Theory

Theorems
8.1
-
8.3
establish useful martingale laws of large numbers.

Theorem 8.1

(Theorem 2.19 from
Hall and Heyde (2014)
)
.

Let
\{X_{n},n\geq 1\}
be a sequence of random variables and
\{\mathcal{F}_{n},n\geq 1\}
be an increasing sequence of
\sigma
-fields such that
X_{n}
is
\mathcal{F}^{n}
measurable for each
n
. Let
X
be a r.v. and
c
a constant such
\mathbb{E}|X|<\infty
,
\mathbb{E}(|X|\log^{+}|X|)<\infty
, and
\mathbb{P}(|X_{n}|>x)\leq c\mathbb{P}(|X|>x)
for each
x\geq 0
and
n\geq 1
(note that if
X_{n}
is bounded, this condition is satisfied). Then

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{X_{i}-\mathbb{E}(X_{i}|\mathcal{F}_{i-1})\right\}\to 0

a.s. as
n\to\infty
.

Theorem 8.2

(Theorem 2.17 from
Hall and Heyde (2014)
)
.

Let
\left\{S_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i},\mathcal{F}_{n},n\geq 1\right\}
be a martingale, and let
1\leq p\leq 2
. Then
S_{n}
