, we reparameterize
\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}=-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{-1}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathbf{a}}}=\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{-1}G^{\dagger}_{{\mathbf{a}}}
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{J}\left(\left\{\Gamma_{{\mathbf{a}}},\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}\right\}_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{J}\prod_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}\prod_{l=1}^{M_{i}}(2\pi)^{-p/2}|\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}-2\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}\mathbb{I}({\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}})|^{1/2}\times

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\exp\Bigg[\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}\Big\{{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i,l}}^{\top}\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i,l}+{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{*}_{i}}^{\top}\Gamma_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{\top}{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i,l}+

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\frac{1}{4}{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{*}_{i}}^{\top}\Gamma_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{\top}{\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}}^{-1}\Gamma_{{\mathbf{a}}}{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{*}_{i}\Big\}\mathbb{I}({\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}})\Bigg].

The reparameterized log-likelihood is

\displaystyle\mathcal{\ell}_{J}\left(\left\{\Gamma_{{\mathbf{a}}},\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}\right\}_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}\right)

\displaystyle\triangleq\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}\sum_{l=1}^{M_{i}}\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}\Bigg[(-p/2)\log(2\pi)+\frac{1}{2}\log|-2\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}|+

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i,l}}^{\top}\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i,l}+{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{*}_{i}}^{\top}\Gamma_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{\top}{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i,l}+

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\frac{1}{4}{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{*}_{i}}^{\top}\Gamma_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{\top}{\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}}^{-1}\Gamma_{{\mathbf{a}}}{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{*}_{i}\Bigg]\mathbb{I}({\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}\sum_{l=1}^{M_{i}}\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}\Bigg[(-p/2)\log(2\pi)+\frac{1}{2}\log|-2\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}|+

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\mathrm{tr}\left(\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i,l}{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i,l}}^{\top}\right)+\mathrm{tr}\left(\Gamma_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{\top}{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i,l}{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{*}_{i}}^{\top}\right)+

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\mathrm{tr}\left(\frac{1}{4}\Gamma_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{\top}{\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}}^{-1}\Gamma_{{\mathbf{a}}}{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{*}_{i}{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{*}_{i}}^{\top}\right)\Bigg]\mathbb{I}({\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}}),

where the second equality follows from rearranging terms and using the properties of the trace operator.
For
{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}
, define
n_{{\mathbf{a}}}\triangleq\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}M_{i}\mathbb{I}({\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}})
and
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}^{J}_{{\mathbf{a}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{(p+1)\times(p+1)}
such that
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}^{J}_{{\mathbf{a}}}\triangleq\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}M_{i}{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{*}_{i}{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{*}_{i}}^{\top}\mathbb{I}({\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}})
. We apply the differential operator two times and find

\displaystyle{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}}^{2}\mathcal{\ell}_{J}\left(\left\{\Gamma_{{\mathbf{a}}},\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}\right\}_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}\sum_{l=1}^{M_{i}}\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}-{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}}^{2}\Bigg\{-\frac{1}{2}\log|-2\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}|-

\displaystyle\hskip 76.82234pt\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{tr}\left(\Gamma_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{\top}{\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}}^{-1}\Gamma_{{\mathbf{a}}}{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{*}_{i}{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{*}_{i}}^{\top}\right)\Bigg\}\mathbb{I}({\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}})

\displaystyle=-{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}}\Bigg\{-\frac{n_{{\mathbf{a}}}}{2}\mathrm{tr}\left(\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{-1}{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}}\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}\right)-\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{tr}\left(2\Gamma_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{\top}\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{-1}{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}}\Gamma_{{\mathbf{a}}}{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}^{J}_{{\mathbf{a}}}\right)+

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{tr}\left(\Gamma_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{\top}{\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}}^{-1}{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}}\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}{\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}}^{-1}\Gamma_{{\mathbf{a}}}{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}^{J}_{{\mathbf{a}}}\right)\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=-\Bigg\{\frac{n_{{\mathbf{a}}}}{2}\mathrm{tr}\left(\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{-1}{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}}\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{-1}{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}}\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}\right)-

\displaystyle\hskip 34.14322pt\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}\left(\Gamma_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{\top}\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{-1}{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}}\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{-1}{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}}\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{-1}\Gamma_{{\mathbf{a}}}{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}^{J}_{{\mathbf{a}}}\right)-

\displaystyle\hskip 34.14322pt\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}\left({\mbox{\boldmath$d$}}\Gamma_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{\top}\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{-1}{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}}\Gamma_{{\mathbf{a}}}{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}^{J}_{{\mathbf{a}}}\right)+

\displaystyle\hskip 34.14322pt\mathrm{tr}\left(\Gamma_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{\top}\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{-1}{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}}\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{-1}{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}}\Gamma_{{\mathbf{a}}}{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}^{J}_{{\mathbf{a}}}\right)+

\displaystyle\hskip 34.14322pt\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{tr}\left(\Gamma_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{\top}{\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}}^{-1}{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}}\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}{\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}}^{-1}\Gamma_{{\mathbf{a}}}{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}^{J}_{{\mathbf{a}}}\right)\Bigg\}.

We observe that for
{\mathbf{a}},{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
and
{\mathbf{a}}\neq{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}
,

\frac{\partial\mathcal{\ell}_{J}\left(\left\{\Gamma_{{\mathbf{a}}},\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}\right\}_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}\right)}{\partial\left(\mathrm{vec}(\Gamma_{{\mathbf{a}}}),\mathrm{vec}(\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}})\right)\partial\left(\mathrm{vec}(\Gamma_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}),\mathrm{vec}(\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}})\right)^{\top}}=[0]_{(p+1)^{2}\times p^{2}}.

We express

\displaystyle\ddot{\ell}_{J}\left(\left\{\Gamma_{{\mathbf{a}}},\Omega_{{\mathbf{a}}}\right\}_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}\right)=

\displaystyle-\mathrm{diag}\left[\left\{\begin{pmatrix}{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}^{J}_{{\mathbf{a}}}\otimes\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}&2\left({\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}^{J}_{{\mathbf{a}}}{G^{*}_{{\mathbf{a}}}}^{\top}\otimes\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}\right)\\
2\left(G^{*}_{{\mathbf{a}}}{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}^{J}_{{\mathbf{a}}}\otimes\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}\right)&4\left(G^{*}_{{\mathbf{a}}}{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}^{J}_{{\mathbf{a}}}{G^{*}_{{\mathbf{a}}}}^{\top}\otimes\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}\right)+2n_{{\mathbf{a}}}\left(\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}\otimes\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}\right)\end{pmatrix}\right\}_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}\right]

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{diag}\left[\left\{\begin{pmatrix}{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}^{J}_{{\mathbf{a}}}&2{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}^{J}_{{\mathbf{a}}}{G^{*}_{{\mathbf{a}}}}^{\top}\\
2G^{*}_{{\mathbf{a}}}{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}^{J}_{{\mathbf{a}}}&4G^{*}_{{\mathbf{a}}}{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}^{J}_{{\mathbf{a}}}{G^{*}_{{\mathbf{a}}}}^{\top}+2n_{{\mathbf{a}}}\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}\end{pmatrix}\otimes\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}\right\}_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}\right].

The second equality follows from properties of Kronecker products.

The Hessian for the log-likelihood of the reward model.

The estimating equation for the reward model parameter,
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y}
, is

\frac{\partial\ell_{J}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y})}{\partial{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y}}=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}\sum_{l=1}^{M_{i}}\left[Y_{i,l}{\mbox{\boldmath$Z$}}_{i,l}-\left\{\frac{1}{1+\exp(-{{\mbox{\boldmath$Z$}}_{i,l}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y})}*{\mbox{\boldmath$Z$}}_{i,l}\right\}\right].

The derivative of the estimating equation (the hessian of the log-likelihood) is

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}\ell_{J}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y})}{\partial{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y}\partial{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y}^{\top}}=-\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}\sum_{l=1}^{M_{i}}{\mbox{\boldmath$Z$}}_{i,l}{{\mbox{\boldmath$Z$}}_{i,l}}^{\top}\left\{\frac{1}{1+\exp(-{{\mbox{\boldmath$Z$}}_{i,l}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y})}\right\}\left\{\frac{1}{1+\exp\left({{\mbox{\boldmath$Z$}}_{i,l}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y}\right)}\right\}.

The Hessian for the log-likelihood of the arrival model.

Appealing to Equation
26
, the estimating equation for the arrival model parameter,
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t}=\zeta
, is

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\ell_{J}(\zeta)}{\partial\zeta}=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}\sum_{l=1}^{M_{i}}\frac{1}{\zeta}-U_{i,l}-\frac{t_{\min}e^{-\zeta t_{\min}}-t_{\max}e^{-\zeta t_{\max}}}{e^{-\zeta t_{\min}}-e^{-\zeta t_{\max}}}.

The derivative of the estimating equation (the hessian of the log-likelihood) is

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}\ell_{J}(\zeta)}{\partial\zeta^{2}}=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}-M_{i}\left(\frac{1}{\zeta^{2}}+\frac{t_{\min}e^{-\zeta t_{\min}}-t_{\max}^{2}e^{-\zeta t_{\max}}}{[e^{-\zeta t_{\min}}-e^{-\zeta t_{\max}}]^{2}}\right).

The Hessian for the log-likelihood of the family model.

We reparameterize the log-likelihood in Equation
26
, where
\tau=\log(\lambda)
:

\ell_{J}(\tau)=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}\left[M_{i}\tau-\log\{\sum_{\ell=k}^{|\mathbf{A}_{i}|}(e^{\tau\ell}/\ell!)\}\right].

The score function is

\frac{\partial\ell_{J}(\tau)}{\partial\tau}=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}\left[M_{i}-\tau\frac{\sum_{\ell=k}^{|\mathbf{A}_{i}|}\ell e^{\tau\ell}/\ell!)}{\sum_{\ell=k}^{|\mathbf{A}_{i}|}e^{\tau\ell}/\ell!}\right].

This makes the Hessian of the log-likelihood

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}\ell_{J}(\tau)}{\partial\tau^{2}}=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}-\tau^{2}\frac{\left\{\sum_{\ell=k}^{|\mathbf{A}_{i}|}e^{\tau\ell}/\ell!\right\}\left\{\sum_{\ell=k}^{|\mathbf{A}_{i}|}\ell^{2}e^{\tau\ell}/\ell!\right\}-\left\{\sum_{\ell=k}^{|\mathbf{A}_{i}|}\ell e^{\tau\ell}/\ell!\right\}^{2}}{\left\{\sum_{\ell=k}^{|\mathbf{A}_{i}|}e^{\tau\ell}/\ell!\right\}^{2}}.

11.2
Verification of Assumptions
4
-
5
and
7
-
8

11.2.1
Assumption
4

Assumption
4
is satisfied by Thompson sampling with a clipping constraint. We show this in Lemma
11.1
.

Lemma 11.1
.

For any
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}
, assume
{\mathbf{A}}_{i}
is assigned by RL-RDS with a clipping constraint at level
\epsilon_{1}\in(0,1)
(as specified in Section
2
). Additionally, assume Conditions (C1)-(C6). There exists
\epsilon_{\min},\epsilon_{\max}>0
such that for all
{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}
,

\epsilon_{\min}\leq\mathbb{P}({\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}}|{\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}_{i})\leq\epsilon_{\max}\ \ \mathrm{w.p.\ 1}.

This implies that for
\rho_{\min}=\sqrt{\epsilon_{\min}}
and
\rho_{\max}=\sqrt{\epsilon_{\max}}
,

\rho_{\min}\leq{\mbox{\boldmath$W$}}_{i}\leq\rho_{\max}\ \ \mathrm{w.p.\ 1}.

Proof.

Note that by Condition (C3), the set of possible coupon allocations is constant for the duration of the study.
For any
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}
, Thompson sampling with clipping at level
\epsilon_{1}\in(0,1)

selects allocation
{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}

with probability

\mathbb{P}\left({\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}}\right)=\frac{p_{i}^{{\mathbf{a}}}}{\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}p_{i}^{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}},

where
p^{{\mathbf{a}}}_{i}\triangleq\min\left[1-\epsilon_{1},\max\left\{\epsilon_{1},\widehat{\xi}^{i}_{B}({\mbox{\boldmath$H$}}_{i},{\mathbf{a}})\right\}\right]
(refer to Section
2
for the definition of
\widehat{\xi}^{i}_{B}
). This implies that

\mathbb{P}\left({\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}}\right)=\frac{p_{i}^{{\mathbf{a}}}}{\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}p_{i}^{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}}\geq\frac{\epsilon_{1}}{(1-\epsilon_{1})|\mathcal{A}|}.

Consequently, for any
{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}
,

\frac{1-\epsilon_{1}}{1+(|\mathcal{A}|-2)\epsilon_{1}}\geq\mathbb{P}\left({\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}}\right).

The inequality follows from the fact that
1-\epsilon_{1}\geq p_{i}^{{\mathbf{a}}}\geq\epsilon_{1}
.
We define

\epsilon_{\min}\triangleq\epsilon_{1}/\left\{(1-\epsilon_{1})|\mathcal{A}|\right\},\ \ \epsilon_{\max}\triangleq\frac{1-\epsilon_{1}}{1+(|\mathcal{A}|-2)\epsilon_{1}}.

\blacksquare

11.2.2
Assumption
5

Assumption
5
is satisfied by Equation
7
(in Section
2.1
of the main text) and Condition (C1). See the verification of Assumption
11
for a further discussion of Equation
7
.

11.2.3
Assumptions
7
and
8

Assumption
7
is verified by the derivatives calculated in Section
11.1
and the fact that each component of this branching model is a full rank exponential family. Consequently, the natural parameters are identifiable
(
Brown, 1986
)
.
Assumption
8
follows from the score and Hessian functions calculated in Section
11.1
. Observe that the likelihood and Hessian components are continuous and finite in the data for a given parameter value. Consequently, the fact that the data are bounded implies that for any
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}
,
\dot{l}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}})
and
\ddot{l}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}})
are bounded (by the extreme value theorem). This implies that the conditional expectations of these functions are bounded and
Assumption
8
is satisfied.

11.3
Verification of Assumptions
9
and
11
-
14

We now verify Assumptions
9
and
11
-
14
.

11.3.1
Note on Assumption
10

Note that Assumption
10
is only used in the proof of consistency in Section
8
. It is unnecessary because the log-likelihood of the branching process specified in Equation
6
is concave. We will provide another proof of consistency in Section
11.4
that does not use Assumption
10
.

11.3.2
Supporting Lemmas

Lemmas
11.2
and
11.3
are useful properties of positive semi-definite matrices.

Lemma 11.2
.

Define positive semi-definite matrices
A,B\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
. It follows that

\sigma_{\min}(A+B)\geq\sigma_{\min}(A)+\sigma_{\min}(B).

Proof.

\displaystyle\sigma_{\min}(A+B)

\displaystyle=\min_{x\neq 0}\frac{x^{\top}(A+B)x}{x^{\top}x}

\displaystyle=\min_{x\neq 0}\left(\frac{x^{\top}Ax}{x^{\top}x}+\frac{x^{\top}Bx}{x^{\top}x}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\min_{x\neq 0}\frac{x^{\top}Ax}{x^{\top}x}+\min_{x\neq 0}\frac{x^{\top}Bx}{x^{\top}x}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\min}(A)+\sigma_{\min}(B).

The first line follows from the definition of the minimum singular value for positive semi-definite matrices, and the second from distributing. For any functions
f
and
g
,
\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\left\{f(x)+g(x)\right\}\geq\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}}f(x)+\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}}g(x)
. Consequently, line 3 is true. Line 4 applies the definiton of the minimum singular value again.

\blacksquare

Lemma 11.3
.

Let
A,B\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be positive semi-definite matrices. It follows that

\sigma_{\min}(A)\sigma_{\min}(B)\leq\sigma_{\min}(AB).

Proof.

First, we assume that martices
A
and
B
are invertible.
By the sub-multiplicativity of the spectral norm, we know that

\sigma_{\max}(AB)\leq\sigma_{\max}(A)\sigma_{\max}(B).

Consequently,

\displaystyle\sigma_{\max}\{(AB)^{-1}\}\leq\sigma_{\max}(A^{-1})\sigma_{\max}(B^{-1})\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\sigma_{\min}(AB)^{-1}\leq\sigma_{\min}(A)^{-1}\sigma_{\min}(B)^{-1}\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\sigma_{\min}(A)\sigma_{\min}(B)\leq\sigma_{\min}(AB).

The first line applies the sub-multiplicativity of the spectral norm. For any matrix
A
,
\sigma_{\max}(A^{-1})=\sigma_{\min}(A)^{-1}
. Consequently, line 2 is true.

If matrices
A
and
B
are not invertible, then
\sigma_{\min}(A)=0
,
\sigma_{\min}(B)=0
, and
\sigma_{\min}(AB)=0
. Therefore, the result follows trivially.

\blacksquare

Lemma 11.4
.

Let
M\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be a matrix such that

\displaystyle M=\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
B^{\top}&C\end{pmatrix}

where
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{1}}
and
C\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}\times d_{2}}
are symmetric and
d_{1}+d_{2}=d
.

M
is positive definite if and only if
A
is positive definite and
C-B^{\top}A^{-1}B
is positive definite. Additionally,

\sigma_{\min}(M)\geq\sigma_{\min}(A)\sigma_{\min}(C-B^{\top}A^{-1}B).

Proof.

Define
I_{d_{1}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{1}}
and
I_{d_{2}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}\times d_{2}}
as the
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
-dimensional identity matrices respectively.
We express
M
as

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
B^{\top}&C\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}I_{d_{1}}&0\\
B^{\top}A^{-1}&I_{d_{2}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}A&0\\
0&C-B^{\top}A^{-1}B\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}I_{d_{1}}&A^{-1}B\\
0&I_{d_{2}}\end{pmatrix}.

Because

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}I_{d_{1}}&0\\
B^{\top}A^{-1}&I_{d_{2}}\end{pmatrix}

and its transpose are invertible, we know that
M
is positive definite if and only if

\displaystyle Q=\begin{pmatrix}A&0\\
0&C-B^{\top}A^{-1}B\end{pmatrix}

is positive definite. The matrix
Q
is block diagonal, so it is positive definite if and only if

A
and
C-B^{\top}A^{-1}B
are positive definite. Additionally, by Lemma
11.3
,

\displaystyle\sigma_{\min}(M)

\displaystyle\geq\sigma_{\min}\left\{\begin{pmatrix}I_{d_{1}}&0\\
B^{\top}A^{-1}&I_{d_{2}}\end{pmatrix}\right\}\sigma_{\min}\left\{\begin{pmatrix}A&0\\
0&C-B^{\top}A^{-1}B\end{pmatrix}\right\}\times

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\sigma_{\min}\left\{\begin{pmatrix}I_{d_{1}}&A^{-1}B\\
0&I_{d_{2}}\end{pmatrix}\right\}

\displaystyle\geq\sigma_{\min}(A)\sigma_{\min}\left(C-B^{\top}A^{-1}B\right)\sigma_{\min}\left(I_{d_{1}}\right)^{2}\sigma_{\min}\left(I_{d_{2}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\min}(A)\sigma_{\min}\left(C-B^{\top}A^{-1}B\right)

\blacksquare

11.3.3
Assumptions
9
and
14

We verify
Assumption
9
first. We write
\dot{l}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})=\dot{l}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*},{\mbox{\boldmath$D$}}_{i})
as a function
\dot{l}:\mathcal{B}\times\mathscr{D}\to\mathbb{R}
. From Section
11.2
, we know that
\dot{l}
is continuous over both
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathscr{D}
. Because
\mathcal{X}
is compact (and the other data types are bounded by definition), we know that
\mathscr{D}
is compact. By the extreme value theorem, we can define
\gamma<\infty
,

\max_{{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}}\in\mathscr{D}}\max_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\in\mathcal{B}}\left\|\dot{l}({\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}})\right\|_{2}\leq\gamma.

\mathcal{B}
is a convex subset of
\mathbb{R}^{q}
. Consequently, by the mean value theorem, we know that for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}
, and
{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}
,

\left|l_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}})-l_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime})\right|\leq|\dot{l}_{i}(\bar{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}})|\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}\|_{2},

for some
\bar{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}=t{\boldsymbol{\beta}}+(1-t){\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}
where
t\in[0,1]
. Because
|\dot{l}_{i}(\bar{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}})|=|\dot{l}(\bar{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}},{\mbox{\boldmath$D$}}_{i})|\leq\gamma
, we know that

\left|l_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}})-l_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime})\right|\leq\gamma\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}\|_{2}

and
Assumptions
9
is satisfied.

The likelihood of the arrival process implied by data
\mathcal{D}^{\kappa}=\left\{(R^{v},T^{v},{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{v},Y^{v},{\mathbf{A}}^{v})\right\}_{v=1}^{\kappa}
is simply a censored (or integrated) version of the complete generation likelihood of Section
11.2
.
Consequently, write
\dot{q}^{v}({\boldsymbol{\beta}})=\dot{q}({\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\mbox{\boldmath$D$}}^{v})
(the components of this likelihood as defined in Section
2
) as a function
\dot{q}:\mathcal{B}\times\mathscr{D}\to\mathbb{R}
, we know that
\dot{q}
is continuous over both
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathscr{D}
. Therefore,
Assumption
14
is satisfied by the logic presented in the previous paragraph.

11.3.4
Assumption
11

In this section, we verify
Assumption
11
. To do this, we introduce the branching process in a manner consistent with
Delmas and Marsalle (2010)
. Most of the following setup is taken directly from their paper; we reproduce it here for the reader’s convenience.
We modify the background and theory where necessary to generalize their proofs to more than two possible recruits (or cells in their case).

Let
\mathbb{G}_{0}=\emptyset
,
{\mathbb{G}}_{j}=\{1,\ldots,L\}^{j}
for
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbb{T}_{r}=\cup_{0\leq j\leq r}\mathbb{G}_{j}
. The set
{\mathbb{G}}_{j}
contains all possible recruits in the
j
-th generation. Note that for participant
i\in{\mathbb{G}}_{j}
,
j=|i|
denotes the generation of
i
.
Additionally, for
i,j\in{\mathbb{T}}
, we can represent the concatenation of their positions as
ij=(i,j)
.
For recruit
i\in\mathbb{T}
, we still denote
{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
as an individual’s covariates as specified in the model described by Equation
6
. We define the transition kernel implied by this covariate process over the branching tree as follows.
Let
(E,\mathcal{E})
be a measurable space, and let
P
be a probability kernel on
E\times\mathcal{E}^{L}
with values in
[0,1]
. This implies that
P(\cdot,A)
is measurable for all
A\in\mathcal{E}^{L}
and
P({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}},\cdot)
is a probability measure on
(E^{L},\mathcal{E}^{L})
. Additionally, for any real-valued function
g
defined on
E^{L}
and
{\mbox{\boldmath$q$}}=({\mbox{\boldmath$q$}}_{1},{\mbox{\boldmath$q$}}_{2},\ldots,{\mbox{\boldmath$q$}}_{L})
, we set

Pg({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}})=\int_{E^{L}}g({\mbox{\boldmath$q$}})P({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}},d{\mbox{\boldmath$q$}}).

We call a stochastic process indexed by
{\mathbb{T}}
,
\left\{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i},i\in{\mathbb{T}}\right\}
, a fixed branching Markov chain on a measurable space
(E,\mathcal{E})
with initial distribution
\nu
and probability kernel
P
if:

•

{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{\emptyset}
is distributed as
\nu

•

It satisfies a Markov property: for any measureable real-valued bounded functions
(g_{i},i\in{\mathbb{T}})
defined on
E^{L}
, we have that for all
j\geq 0

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i\in{\mathbb{G}}_{j}}g_{i}\left({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i1},{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i2},\ldots,{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{iL}\right)|\sigma({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{k},k\in{\mathbb{T}}_{j})\right]=\prod_{i\in{\mathbb{G}}_{j}}Pg_{i}\left({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}\right).

We consider the metric measurable space
\left(S,\mathcal{S}\right)
, and add a cemetery point to
S
,
\delta
. Let
\overline{S}=\mathbb{R}^{p}\cup\{\delta\}
and
\overline{\mathcal{S}}
be the
\sigma
-field generated by
\mathcal{S}
and
\{\delta\}
. Let
P^{*}
be a probability kernel defined on
\overline{S}\times\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{L}
such that

P^{*}\left(\delta,\left\{(\delta,\delta,\ldots,\delta)\right\}\right)=1.

(28)

Note that this condition means that
\delta
is an absorbing state.

The covariate process specified in Equation
6
,
\{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i},i\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*}\}
with
{\mathbb{T}}^{*}=\left\{i\in{\mathbb{T}}:{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}\neq\delta\right\}
is a fixed branching Markov chain on
(\overline{S},\overline{\mathcal{S}})
with
P^{*}
satisfying Equation
28
. Note that the recruit covariate distributions are independently and identically distributed. In other words, for
V\triangleq(V_{1}\times V_{2}\times\cdots\times V_{L})\in\mathcal{S}^{L}
and an induced kernel
P_{1}
,

\int_{V}P({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}},{\mbox{\boldmath$q$}})d{\mbox{\boldmath$q$}}=\int_{V^{1}}\int_{V^{2}}\cdots\int_{V^{L}}P_{1}({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}},d{\mbox{\boldmath$q$}}_{1})P_{1}({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}},d{\mbox{\boldmath$q$}}_{2})\cdots P_{1}({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}},d{\mbox{\boldmath$q$}}_{L})d{\mbox{\boldmath$q$}}_{1}d{\mbox{\boldmath$q$}}_{2}\cdots d{\mbox{\boldmath$q$}}_{L}.

Additionally, the family size distribution is only dependent on the total number of recruits. This fact, along with the i.i.d. property described above, implies that, for
\overline{V}=(\overline{V}^{1},\overline{V}^{2},\ldots,\overline{V}^{L})\in\overline{S}^{L}
,

\displaystyle\int_{\overline{V}}P^{*}({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}},{\mbox{\boldmath$q$}})d{\mbox{\boldmath$q$}}

\displaystyle=\int_{V^{1}}\int_{V^{2}}\cdots\int_{V^{L}}P^{*}({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}},{\mbox{\boldmath$q$}})d{\mbox{\boldmath$q$}}

\displaystyle=\int_{V^{z(1)}}\int_{V^{z(2)}}\cdots\int_{V^{z(L)}}P^{*}({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}},{\mbox{\boldmath$q$}})d{\mbox{\boldmath$q$}}

for any permutation
z
of
\{1,2,\ldots,L\}
. We will call this property the “exchangeability” of
P^{*}
.
Lastly, the process is “spatially homogeneous;” i.e., for
\overline{V}\in\left\{S,\delta\right\}^{L}
and
{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}_{1},{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}_{2}\in S
,

P^{*}\left({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}_{1},\overline{V}\right)=P^{*}\left({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}_{2},\overline{V}\right).

In other words, the family size distribution does not depend on the covariate value of the parent.
By Condition (C1), the Galton-Watson (GW) tree implied by this process is super-critical.

We now cite some well known results from the branching process literature
(
Athreya et al., 2004
)
. For any subset
O\subset{\mathbb{T}}
, let

O^{*}=O\cap{\mathbb{T}}^{*}=\left\{o\in O,{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{k}\neq\delta\right\}

be the subset of
O
that are “realized” recruits. Labeling
Z_{j}\triangleq|{\mathbb{G}}_{j}^{*}|
,
\left\{Z_{j},j\in\mathbb{N}\right\}
is a GW process. For
j\geq 0
, we know

\mathbb{E}\left(\left|{\mathbb{G}}_{j}^{*}\right|\right)=m^{j}.

And, for
r\geq 0
, we know

\mathbb{E}\left(\left|{\mathbb{T}}^{*}_{r}\right|\right)=\sum_{j=0}^{r}\mathbb{E}\left(\left|{\mathbb{G}}_{j}^{*}\right|\right)=\sum_{j=0}^{r}m^{j}=\frac{m^{r+1}-1}{m-1}.

Additionally, there exists a random variable
\mathscr{I}
s.t.

\mathscr{I}=\lim_{j\to\infty}m^{-j}\left|{\mathbb{G}}_{j}^{*}\right|\quad\mathrm{a.s.\ and\ in}\ L^{2}

(29)

where
\mathbb{E}\left(\mathscr{I}\right)=1
.

We now define a series of useful sub-probability kernels. The first is on
S\times\mathcal{S}^{2}
such that

P^{*}_{2}=P^{*}\left\{\cdot,\left(\cdot\cap S^{2}\right)\times\overline{S}^{L-2}\right\}.

Note that by the exchangeability of
P^{*}
, for any
i,j\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,L-2\right\}
such that
i+j\leq L-2
,

P^{*}_{2}=P^{*}\left\{\cdot,\left(\cdot\cap S^{2}\right)\times\overline{S}^{L-2}\right\}=P^{*}\left\{\cdot,\overline{S}^{i}\times\left(\cdot\cap S\right)\times\overline{S}^{j}\times\left(\cdot\cap S\right)\times\overline{S}^{L-2-i-j}\right\}.

The second sub-probability kernel is defined on
S\times\mathcal{S}
in the following manner,

P^{*}_{1}=P^{*}\left\{\cdot,\left(\cdot\cap S\right)\times\overline{S}^{L-1}\right\}.

By the same exchangeability property, we know that for any
0\leq i\leq L-1
,

P^{*}_{1}=P^{*}\left\{\cdot,\left(\cdot\cap S\right)\times\overline{S}^{L-1}\right\}=P^{*}\left\{\cdot,\overline{S}^{i}\times\left(\cdot\cap S\right)\times\overline{S}^{L-1-i}\right\}.

We introduce an auxiliary Markov chain. Let
\left\{{\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{j},j\in\mathbb{N}\right\}
be a Markov chain on
S
with
{\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{0}
distributed as
{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{\emptyset}
and transition kernel

Q=\frac{L}{m}P_{1}^{*}.

The distribution of
{\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{j}
corresponds to the distribution of
{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{I}
conditional on
\{I\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*}\}
, where
I
is chosen at random from
{\mathbb{G}}_{j}
.

We explore the nature of transition kernel
Q
as it will be useful in theory later. We define
P_{1}
as a probability kernel on
S\times\mathcal{S}
that represents the marginal of
P
(note the use of
P
instead of
P^{*}
here). Consequently, it represents the dynamics of a single recruiter, recruit covariate process
(with the guarantee that the recruit exists). Formally,
P_{1}(\cdot,V)
is measurable for
V\in\mathcal{S}
, where

P_{1}(\cdot,V)=P(\cdot,V\times S^{L-1}),

and
P_{1}({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}},\cdot)
is a probability measurable on
\left(S,\mathcal{S}\right)
such that

P_{1}({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}},\cdot)=P({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}},\cdot\times S^{L-1}).

We can also define
P_{1}
as the sub-probability kernel
P_{1}^{*}
re-normalized over
S
. We see that for
{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\in S
,

P_{1}({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}},\cdot)=P^{*}_{1}({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}},\cdot|\cdot\in S)=\frac{P_{1}^{*}({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}},\cdot)}{P_{1}^{*}({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}},S)},

or in other words
P_{1}=P_{1}^{*}/P_{1}^{*}({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}},S)
. We now show that

Q=\frac{L}{m}P_{1}^{*}({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}},S)=P_{1}^{*}/P_{1}^{*}({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}},S)=P_{1},

where
Q
is defined as the transition kernel for the auxiliary Markov chain
({\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{j},j\in\mathbb{N})
above.

Lemma 11.5
.

Let
Q
be defined as above, then

Q=\frac{L}{m}P_{1}^{*}=P^{*}_{1}/P_{1}^{*}({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}},S)=P_{1}

Proof.

To prove the following statement, we need to show that

\frac{m}{L}=P_{1}^{*}({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}},S).

Due to the spatial homogeneity of the Markov process, we can define
p_{\gamma}
for

\gamma\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,L\right\}
as the probability that
\gamma
people are recruited (regardless of the recruiter’s covariates). We evaluate
P_{1}^{*}({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}},S)
in terms of these probabilities (keeping in mind that the recruits are identically distributed),

\displaystyle P_{1}^{*}({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}},S)

\displaystyle=\frac{p_{1}}{L!/\left\{(L-1)!1!\right\}}+\frac{(L-1)!/\left\{(L-2)!1!\right\}p_{2}}{L!/\left\{(L-2)!2!\right\}}+

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{(L-1)!/\left\{(L-3)!2!\right\}p_{3}}{L!/\left\{(L-3)!3!\right\}}+\cdots+p_{L}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{L}\frac{ip_{i}}{L}

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{L}.

\blacksquare

If
(E,\mathcal{E})
is a metric measurable space, then define
\mathcal{B}_{b}(E)
(resp.
\mathcal{B}_{+}(E)
) to be the set of bounded (resp. non-negative) real-valued measurable functions on
E
. The set
\mathcal{C}_{b}(E)
(resp.
C_{+}(E)
) denotes the set of bounded (resp. non-negative) real-valued continuous functions defined on
E
. For a finite measure
\lambda
on
\left(E,\mathcal{E}\right)
and
f\in\mathcal{B}_{b}(E)\cup\mathcal{B}_{+}(E)
we shall write

\langle\lambda,f\rangle=\int f({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}})d\lambda({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}).

Additionally, we write
\mathbb{E}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}
when
{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{\emptyset}={\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}
. We end this preamble with the definition of ergodicity for the Markov chain
\left\{{\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{j},j\in\mathbb{N}\right\}
.

Definition 11.1
.

The Markov chain
\left\{{\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{j},j\in\mathbb{N}\right\}
is ergodic if there exists a probability measure
\mu
on
\left(S,\mathcal{S}\right)
such that for all
f\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(S)
and all
{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\in S
,

\lim_{j\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}\left\{f({\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{j})\right\}=\langle\mu,f\rangle.

Before tackling the strong law of large numbers for the branching process covariate model, we reproduce a helpful Lemma from
Delmas and Marsalle (2010)
with slight adaptations.

Lemma 11.6
.

For
f\in\mathcal{B}_{b}(S)\cup\mathcal{B}_{+}(S)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\{f({\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{j})\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}m^{-j}\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{G}}_{j}}\mathbb{E}\left\{f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})\mathbb{I}\left(i\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*}\right)\right\}\overset{(b)}{=}\frac{\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{G}}_{j}}f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})\mathbb{I}\left(i\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*}\right)}{\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{G}}_{j}}\mathbb{P}\left(i\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*}\right)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left\{f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{I})\mid I\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*}\right\}.

where
I
is a uniform random variable on
{\mathbb{G}}_{j}
independent of
X
.

Proof.

We consider equality (a). Recall that
{\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{0}
has distribution
\nu
. For
i\in{\mathbb{G}}_{j}
, we know

\mathbb{E}\left\{f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})\mathbb{I}\left(i\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*}\right)\right\}=\mathbb{E}\left\{f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})\mathbb{I}\left({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}\neq\delta\right)\right\}=\langle\nu,(P^{*}_{1})^{j}f\rangle,

following from Equation
28
and the definition of
P^{*}_{1}
.
Consequently,

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{G}}_{j}}\mathbb{E}\left\{f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})\mathbb{I}\left(i\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\{1,2,\ldots,L\}^{j}}\langle\nu,(P^{*}_{1})^{j}f\rangle=\langle\nu,(LP^{*}_{1})^{j}f\rangle=m^{j}\langle\nu,Q^{j}f\rangle

\displaystyle=m^{j}\mathbb{E}\left\{f({\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{j})\right\}.

This gives the first equality. Then take
f=1
in the previous equality to get
m^{j}=\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{G}}_{j}}\mathbb{P}\left(i\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*}\right)
and equality (b). To prove Equality (c), we show by the law of total probability that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\{f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{I})|I\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*}\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{I})\mathbb{I}\left(I\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*}\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(I\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*}\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{G}}_{j}}f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})\mathbb{I}\left(i\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*}\right)\mathbb{P}(I=i)}{\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{G}}_{j}}\mathbb{P}\left(i\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*}\right)\mathbb{P}(I=i)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{G}}_{j}}f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})\mathbb{I}\left(i\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*}\right)}{\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{G}}_{j}}\mathbb{P}\left(i\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*}\right)}.

\blacksquare

We recall that
\nu
denotes the distribution of
{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{\emptyset}
. Any function
f
defined on
S
is extended to
\overline{S}
by
f(\delta)=0
. Let
F
be a vector subspace of
\mathcal{B}(S)
s.t.

1.

F
contains the constants

2.

F^{2}\triangleq\left\{f^{2}:f\in F\right\}\subset F

3.

F\otimes F\subset L^{1}(P({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}},\cdot))
for all
x\in S
and
P\left(f_{0}\otimes f_{1}\right)\in F
for all
f_{0},f_{1}\in F

4.

For
\delta\in[0,1]
,
F\subset L^{1}(P^{*}_{1}({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}},\cdot))
for all
{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\in S
and
P_{1}^{*}(f)\in F
for all
f\in F

5.

There exists a probability measure
\mu
on
(S,\mathcal{S})
such that
F\subset L^{1}(\mu)
and

\lim_{j\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}\left\{f({\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{j})\right\}=\langle\mu,f\rangle
for all
{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\in S
and
f\in F

6.

For all
f\in F
, there exists
g\in F
such that for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
|Q^{j}f|\leq g
.

7.

F\subset L^{1}(\nu)

By convention, a function defined on
\overline{S}
is said to belong to
F
if its restriction to
S
belongs to
F
.

Note that if
{\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{j}
is ergodic and
{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\to P^{*}g
is continuous on
S
for all
g\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(\overline{S}^{2})
then the set
F=\mathcal{C}_{b}(S)
fulfills Properties (1)-(7). Additionally, the stipulation that
{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\to P^{*}g
is continuous on
S
for all
g\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(\overline{S}^{2})
is satisfied if
{\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{j}
has a continuous density (because integrals of continuous functions are continuous).

The Covariate Process of Equation
6
.

First, note that any continuous
f
is automatically bounded since
\mathcal{X}
is bounded. Additionally,
P^{*}g
is an integral over a continuous density, so we know that
{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\to P^{*}g
is continuous on
S
for all
g\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(\overline{S}^{2})
. Consequently, in order for
F
to satisfy Properties (1)-(7) above for the covariate process specified in Equation
6
, we simply require that
F
is the space of continuous functions and
{\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{j}
is Ergodic.

Assume that the probability distributions (and expectations) of this section are with respect to the true branching parameters,
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}
, and the uniform stabilizing policy,
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
.

We now examine the covariate process specified in Equation
6
under
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
, in which the coupon allocations are assigned uniformly at random.
Define
\mathscr{R}^{p}
as the Borel
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\mathbb{R}^{p}
.
For
{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
B\subset\mathscr{R}^{p}
, we know that

P_{1}({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}},B)=Q({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}},B)=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}|}\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}\mathrm{Normal}\left(\boldsymbol{\phi}_{{\mathbf{a}}}+G_{{\mathbf{a}}}{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}},\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}\right)

Consequently, the auxiliary Markov chain
({\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{j},j\in\mathbb{N})
is

f({\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{j}\mid{\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{j-1}={\mbox{\boldmath$k$}}_{j-1})=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}|}\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}\mathrm{Normal}\left(\boldsymbol{\phi}_{{\mathbf{a}}}+G_{{\mathbf{a}}}{\mbox{\boldmath$k$}}_{j-1},\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}\right).

This is a Markov-Switching Autoregressive Model
(
Hamilton, 1989
;
Francq and Zakoıan, 2001
;
Stelzer, 2009
)
. According to
Fong et al. (2007)
, Condition (C6), which states that

\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}|}\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}\log\|G_{{\mathbf{a}}}\|_{2}<0,

implies that the
({\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{j},j\in\mathbb{N})
is Ergodic.

We label the stationary distribution for
{\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{j}
as
\mu
such that for all
{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\in S
,

\lim_{j\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}\left\{f({\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{j})\right\}=\langle\mu,f\rangle.

We lower bound the variance (with the law of total variation) of the stationary distribution,
\mu
,

\mathrm{Var}_{\mu}({\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{j})\succeq\mathrm{Var}_{\mu}\left\{\mathbb{E}\left({\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{j}\mid{\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{j-1}\right)\right\}\succeq\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{A}\right|}\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}.

Define
\Sigma_{{\mbox{\boldmath$k$}}}\triangleq\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{A}\right|}\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}
.

We now prove a Weak Law of Large numbers over the function class
F
. We again follow
Delmas and Marsalle (2010)
with slight additions. Because
({\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{j},j\in\mathbb{N})
is Ergodic and
{\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{j}
has a continuous density for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
, we only need
F=\mathcal{C}_{b}(S)
to satisfy Properties (1)-(7).

Theorem 11.1
.

Let
\{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i},i\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*}\}
be the covariate process specified in Equation
6
.
Let
F=\mathcal{C}_{b}(S)
, which implies it satisfies Properties (1)-(7).
Then,

\left\{\frac{1}{m^{n}}\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{G}}_{j}^{*}}f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}),j\in\mathbb{N}\right\}\to\langle\mu,f\rangle\mathscr{I}

in
L^{2}
as
j\to\infty
, where
\mathscr{I}
is defined by Equation
29
.

Proof.

We first assume that
\langle\mu,f\rangle=0
. We have

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{G}}_{j}^{*}}f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{G}}_{j}^{*}}f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}){\mathbb{I}}(i\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*})\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{G}}_{j}^{*}}\mathbb{E}\left\{f^{2}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}){\mathbb{I}}(i\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*})\right\}+B_{j}

\displaystyle=m^{j}\mathbb{E}\left\{f^{2}({\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{j})\right\}+B_{j},

with
B_{j}=\sum_{(p,q)\in{\mathbb{G}}^{2}_{j},i\neq j}\mathbb{E}\left[f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{p})f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{q}){\mathbb{I}}\left\{(p,q)\in{{\mathbb{T}}^{*}}^{2}\right\}\right]
. We used Lemma
11.6
for the last equality. For
p,q\in{\mathbb{G}}_{j}^{*}
, define
p\wedge q
as the most recent common ancestor of
p
and
q
. We compute
B_{q}
by decomposing the sum according to
k=p\wedge q
:
B_{j}=\sum_{r=0}^{j-1}\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{G}}_{r}}C_{k}
where

C_{k}=\sum_{(p,q)\in{\mathbb{G}}_{j}^{2}.p\wedge q=k}\mathbb{E}\left[f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{p})f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{q}){\mathbb{I}}\left\{(p,q)\in{{\mathbb{T}}^{*}}^{2}\right\}\right].

If
|k|=q-1
, we get

\displaystyle C_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(p,q)\in{\mathbb{G}}_{1}^{2},p\wedge q=\emptyset}\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbb{E}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{k}}\left[f\left({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{kp}\right)f\left({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{kq}\right){\mathbb{I}}\left\{(kp,kq)\in{{\mathbb{T}}^{*}}^{2}\right\}\right]{\mathbb{I}}(k\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*})\right)

\displaystyle=L(L-1)\mathbb{E}\left\{P_{2}^{*}(f\otimes f)({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{k}){\mathbb{I}}(k\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*})\right\}

If
|k|<q-1
, we set
r=|k|
and derive

\displaystyle C_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(w,z)\in G_{1}^{2},w\wedge z=\emptyset}\sum_{(p,q)\in{\mathbb{G}}^{2}_{j-r-1}}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\mathbb{E}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{kw}}\left\{f\left({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{kwp}\right){\mathbb{I}}\left(kwp\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*}\right)\right\}\times

\displaystyle\hskip 156.49014pt\mathbb{E}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{kz}}\left\{f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{kzq}){\mathbb{I}}(kzq\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*})\right\}{\mathbb{I}}(kw\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*},kz\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*})\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(w,z)\in{\mathbb{G}}_{1}^{2},w\wedge z=\emptyset}\mathbb{E}\Bigg(\left[\sum_{p\in{\mathbb{G}}_{j-r-1}}\mathbb{E}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{kw}}\left\{f\left({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{kwp}\right){\mathbb{I}}\left(kwp\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*}\right)\right\}\right]\times

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt\left[\sum_{q\in{\mathbb{G}}_{j-r-1}}\mathbb{E}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{kz}}\left\{f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{kzq}){\mathbb{I}}(kzq\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*})\right\}\right]{\mathbb{I}}(kw\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*},kz\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*})\Bigg)

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}m^{2(j-r-1)}\sum_{(w,z)\in{\mathbb{G}}_{1}^{2},w\wedge z=\emptyset}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\mathbb{E}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{kw}}\left\{f\left({\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{j-r-1}\right)\right\}\times

\displaystyle\hskip 156.49014pt\mathbb{E}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{kz}}\left\{f({\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{j-r-1})\right\}{\mathbb{I}}(kw\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*},kz\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*})\Bigg]

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}m^{2(j-r-1)}\sum_{(w,z)\in{\mathbb{G}}_{1}^{2},w\wedge z=\emptyset}\mathbb{E}\left[Q^{j-r-1}f\left({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{kw}\right)Q^{j-r-1}f\left({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{kz}\right){\mathbb{I}}(kw\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*},kz\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*})\right]

\displaystyle=m^{2(j-r-1)}\sum_{(w,z)\in{\mathbb{G}}_{1}^{2},w\wedge z=\emptyset}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(Q^{j-r-1}f\otimes Q^{j-r-1}f\right)\left({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{kw},{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{kz}\right){\mathbb{I}}(kw\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*},kz\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*})\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{=}m^{2(j-r-1)}\sum_{(w,z)\in{\mathbb{G}}_{1}^{2},w\wedge z=\emptyset}\mathbb{E}\left[P^{*}_{2}\left(Q^{j-r-1}f\otimes Q^{j-r-1}f\right)\left({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{k}\right){\mathbb{I}}(k\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*})\right]

\displaystyle=L(L-1)m^{2(j-r-1)}\mathbb{E}\left[P^{*}_{2}\left(Q^{j-r-1}f\otimes Q^{j-r-1}f\right)\left({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{k}\right){\mathbb{I}}(k\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*})\right]

Equality (a) follows from Lemma
11.6
and (b) through the definition of
Q
. Equality (c) follows from the definition of
P^{*}_{2}
. Consequently, by Lemma
11.6
,

\displaystyle B_{j}

\displaystyle=L(L-1)\sum_{r=0}^{j-1}m^{2(j-r-1)}\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{G}}_{r}}\mathbb{E}\left[P^{*}_{2}\left(Q^{j-r-1}f\otimes Q^{j-r-1}f\right)\left({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{k}\right){\mathbb{I}}(k\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*})\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}L(L-1)\sum_{r=0}^{j-1}m^{2(j-r-1)}m^{r}\langle\nu,Q^{r}P_{2}^{*}\left(Q^{j-r-1}f\otimes Q^{j-r-1}f\right)\rangle

\displaystyle=L(L-1)\sum_{r=0}^{j-1}m^{2j-r-2}\langle\nu,Q^{r}P_{2}^{*}\left(Q^{j-r-1}f\otimes Q^{j-r-1}f\right)\rangle,

where equality (a) follows from Lemma
11.6
. Therefore, we find that

\displaystyle\left\|m^{-j}\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{G}}_{j}^{*}}f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=m^{-2j}\left\|\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{G}}_{j}^{*}}f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=m^{-j}\mathbb{E}\left\{f^{2}({\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{j})\right\}+

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636ptL(L-1)m^{-2}\sum_{r=0}^{q-1}m^{-r}\langle\nu,Q^{r}P^{*}\left(Q^{j-r-1}f\otimes Q^{j-r-1}f\right)\rangle

Because
f\in F
, properties (2), (4), and (6) imply that
E\left\{f^{2}({\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{j})\right\}<\infty
for any
j\in\mathbb{N}
and

\lim_{j\to\infty}m^{-j}\mathbb{E}\left\{f^{2}({\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{j})\right\}=0.

Properties (3), (4), and (5) along with
\langle\mu,f\rangle=0
imply that
P\left(Q^{j-r-1}f\otimes Q^{j-r-1}f\right)
converges to
0
as
j\to\infty
(with
r
fixed) and is bounded uniformly in
j>r
by a function in
F
. Thus, properties (5) and (6) imply that
\langle\nu,Q^{r}P^{*}_{2}\left(Q^{j-r-1}f\otimes Q^{j-r-1}f\right)\rangle
converges to
0
as
j\to\infty
(with
r
fixed) and is bounded uniformly in
j>r
by a fixed constant, say
C
.

For any
\epsilon>0
, we can choose
r_{0}
such that
\sum_{r>r_{0}}m^{-r}C\leq\epsilon/2
. Additionally, choose
j_{0}>r_{0}
such that for
j\geq j_{0}
and
r\leq r_{0}
, we have

\left|\langle\nu,Q^{r}P^{*}_{2}\left(Q^{j-r-1}f\otimes Q^{j-r-1}f\right)\rangle\right|\leq\epsilon/(2r_{0}).

We get that for all
j\geq j_{0}

\sum_{r=0}^{j-1}m^{-r}\left|\langle\nu,Q^{r}P^{*}_{2}\left(Q^{j-r-1}f\otimes Q^{j-r-1}f\right)\rangle\right|\leq\sum_{r=0}^{r_{0}}\epsilon/(2r_{0})+\sum_{r>r_{0}}^{j-1}m^{-r}C\leq\epsilon/2+\epsilon/2=\epsilon.

Therefore,

\lim_{j\to\infty}\sum_{r=0}^{j-1}m^{-r}\langle\nu,Q^{r}P^{*}_{2}\left(Q^{j-r-1}f\otimes Q^{j-r-1}f\right)\rangle=0.

We can now conclude that if
\langle\mu,f\rangle=0
, then

\lim_{j\to\infty}\left\|m^{-j}\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{G}}_{j}^{*}}f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})\right\|_{2}=0.

We consider the case when
\langle\mu,f\rangle
is arbitrary. For any function
f\in F
, we can construct
g=f-\langle\mu,f\rangle
. We decompose as follows,

m^{-j}\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{G}}_{j}^{*}}f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})=m^{-j}\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{G}}_{j}^{*}}g({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})+\langle\mu,f\rangle m^{-j}\left|{\mathbb{G}}_{j}^{*}\right|.

Because
g\in F
and
\langle\mu,g\rangle=0
, we know that

\lim_{j\to\infty}\left\|m^{-j}\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{G}}_{j}^{*}}g({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})\right\|_{2}=0.

Because
\left(m^{-j}\left|{\mathbb{G}}_{j}^{*}\right|,j\geq 1\right)
converges to
L^{2}
(and a.s.) to
\mathscr{I}
, we can conclude that

m^{-j}\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{G}}_{j}^{*}}f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})\overset{L^{2}}{\to}\langle\mu,f\rangle\mathscr{I}

as
j\to\infty
.

\blacksquare

We now prove a similar result for the average over all individuals in
{\mathbb{T}}^{*}_{r}
, the first
r
generations. We set
t_{r}\triangleq\mathbb{E}\left(\left|{\mathbb{T}}_{r}^{*}\right|\right)
and recall that

t_{r}=\frac{m^{r+1}-1}{m-1}.

We now state a lemma that is a direct consequences of Lemma
8.1
.

Lemma 11.7
.

Let
(v_{r},r\in\mathbb{N})
be a sequence of real numbers converging to
a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, and
m
be a real number such that
m>1
. Let

w_{r}=\sum_{j=0}^{r}m^{j-r-1}v_{j}.

Then the sequence
(w_{r},r\in\mathbb{N})
converges to
a/(m-1)
.

Proof.

First we rearrange the sum
w_{r}=\sum_{j=0}^{r}m^{j-r-1}v_{j}
,

w_{r}=\sum_{j=0}^{r}m^{j-r-1}v_{j}=\sum_{j=0}^{r}m^{-j-1}v_{r-j}=\frac{1}{m}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{r}m^{-j}\right)\left(\sum_{j=0}^{r}m^{-j}\right)^{-1}\sum_{j=0}^{r}m^{-j}v_{r-j}.

By Lemma
8.1
,

\lim_{r\to\infty}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{r}m^{-j}\right)^{-1}\sum_{j=0}^{r}m^{-j}v_{r-j}=a.

By the formula for a geometric series,

\lim_{r\to\infty}\sum_{j=0}^{r}m^{-j}=\frac{1}{1-\frac{1}{m}}.

Consequently,

\lim_{r\to\infty}w_{r}=\frac{1}{m}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{r}m^{-j}\right)\left(\sum_{j=0}^{r}m^{-j}\right)^{-1}\sum_{j=0}^{r}m^{-j}v_{r-j}=\frac{1}{m}\frac{1}{1-\frac{1}{m}}a=\frac{a}{m-1}.

\blacksquare

We now state the weak law of large numbers when averaging over the complete tree
{\mathbb{T}}^{*}_{r}
as
r\to\infty
.

Theorem 11.2
.

Let
\{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i},i\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*}\}
be the covariate process specified in Equation
28
. Let
F
satisfy (i)-(vi) and
f\in F
.
Then,

\left\{\frac{1}{t_{r}}\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{T}}_{r}^{*}}f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}),r\in\mathbb{N}\right\}\to\langle\mu,f\rangle\mathscr{I}

in
L^{2}
as
r\to\infty
, where
\mathscr{I}
is defined by Equation
29
.

Proof.

Recalling that

t_{r}=\frac{m^{r+1}-1}{m-1}

we know that

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1}{t_{r}}\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{T}}_{r}^{*}}f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})-\langle\mu,f\rangle\mathscr{I}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\sum_{j=0}^{r}\frac{m^{j}}{t_{r}}\left(\frac{1}{m^{j}}\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{G}}_{j}^{*}}f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})-\langle\mu,f\rangle\mathscr{I}\right)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=0}^{r}\frac{m^{j}}{t_{r}}\left\|\frac{1}{m^{j}}\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{G}}_{j}^{*}}f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})-\langle\mu,f\rangle\mathscr{I}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=m^{r+1}\frac{m-1}{m^{r+1}-1}\sum_{j=0}^{r}m^{j-r-1}\left\|\frac{1}{m^{j}}\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{G}}_{j}^{*}}f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})-\langle\mu,f\rangle\mathscr{I}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{m-1}{1-m^{-r-1}}\sum_{j=0}^{r}m^{j-r-1}\left\|\frac{1}{m^{j}}\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{G}}_{j}^{*}}f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})-\langle\mu,f\rangle\mathscr{I}\right\|_{2}

Consequently,

\left\|\frac{1}{t_{r}}\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{T}}_{r}^{*}}f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})-\langle\mu,f\rangle\mathscr{I}\right\|_{2}\to 0

as
r\to\infty
by Theorem
11.1
and Lemma
11.7
.

\blacksquare

Note that convergence in
L^{2}
implies convergence in probability, so we have proven a weak law of large numbers (WLLN) for the branching covariate process.

Now that we have proven a WLLN for the branching covariate process implied by Equation
6
, we return to the task of verifying Assumption
11
. First, recall that

m=\sum_{m^{v}=1}^{L}m^{v}\frac{\lambda^{m^{v}}/m^{v}!}{\sum_{\ell=0}^{|\mathbf{A}^{v}|}(\lambda^{\ell}/\ell!)}

Additionally, define
\Sigma_{J}=t_{J-1}
.
Note that the differentiability and moment conditions of the log-likelihood imply that

\eta_{J}=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\pi}}}\left\{\dot{l}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})\dot{l}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})^{\top}\mid\mathcal{F}_{j-1}\right\}=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\pi}}}\left\{-\ddot{l}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})\mid\mathcal{F}_{j-1}\right\}.

Consequently, we will use the Hessians calculated in Section
11.2
in the following theory.
We divide
\eta_{J}
into components corresponding to the different models that compose the branching process,
\eta_{J}=\mathrm{diag}\left(\eta_{J}^{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}},\eta_{J}^{y},\eta_{J}^{m},\eta_{J}^{t}\right)
.

First, we analyze the covariate model component,
\eta_{J}^{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}
.

We define quantities

{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{j}^{{\mathbf{a}}}\triangleq\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}M_{i}{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{*}_{i}{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{*}_{i}}^{\top}\mathbb{I}({\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}}),\quad n_{j}^{{\mathbf{a}}}\triangleq\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}M_{i}\mathbb{I}({\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}}),

\eta_{J}^{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}\triangleq\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathrm{diag}\left[\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\pi}}}\left\{\begin{pmatrix}{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{j}^{{\mathbf{a}}}&2{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{j}^{{\mathbf{a}}}{G^{*}_{{\mathbf{a}}}}^{\top}\\
2G^{*}_{{\mathbf{a}}}{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{j}^{{\mathbf{a}}}&4G^{*}_{{\mathbf{a}}}{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{j}^{{\mathbf{a}}}{G^{*}_{{\mathbf{a}}}}^{\top}+2n_{j}^{{\mathbf{a}}}\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{*}\end{pmatrix}\otimes\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{*}\mid\mathcal{F}_{j-1}\right\}_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}\right].

We define

\displaystyle Q_{j,{\mathbf{a}}}^{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}\triangleq\mathbb{E}\left\{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}M_{i}{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{*}_{i}{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{*}_{i}}^{\top}\mathbb{I}({\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}})\mid\mathcal{F}_{j-1}\right\}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}m{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{*}_{i}{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{*}_{i}}^{\top},

and

\displaystyle Q_{j,{\mathbf{a}}}^{{\mathbf{a}}}\triangleq\mathbb{E}\left\{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}M_{i}\mathbb{I}({\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}})\mid\mathcal{F}_{j-1}\right\}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}|}m\kappa_{j-1}.

Recall that

t_{J}=\frac{m^{J+1}-1}{m-1}.

Then

\displaystyle{\Sigma_{J}}^{-1/2}\eta_{J}^{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}{\Sigma_{J}}^{-1/2}=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{J}{\Sigma_{J}}^{-1/2}\mathrm{diag}\left[\left\{\begin{pmatrix}Q_{j,{\mathbf{a}}}^{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}&2Q_{j,{\mathbf{a}}}^{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}{G^{*}_{{\mathbf{a}}}}^{\top}\\
2G^{*}_{{\mathbf{a}}}Q_{j,{\mathbf{a}}}^{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}&4G^{*}_{{\mathbf{a}}}Q_{j,{\mathbf{a}}}^{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}{G^{*}_{{\mathbf{a}}}}^{\top}+2Q_{j,{\mathbf{a}}}^{{\mathbf{a}}}\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{*}\end{pmatrix}\otimes\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{*}\right\}_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}\right]{\Sigma_{J}}^{-1/2}=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathrm{diag}\left[\left\{\begin{pmatrix}Q_{j,{\mathbf{a}}}^{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}&2Q_{j,{\mathbf{a}}}^{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}{G^{*}_{{\mathbf{a}}}}^{\top}\\
2G^{*}_{{\mathbf{a}}}Q_{j,{\mathbf{a}}}^{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}&4G^{*}_{{\mathbf{a}}}Q_{j,{\mathbf{a}}}^{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}{G^{*}_{{\mathbf{a}}}}^{\top}+2Q_{j,{\mathbf{a}}}^{{\mathbf{a}}}\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{*}\end{pmatrix}\otimes\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{*}\right\}_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}\right]/t_{J-1}.

We know that

\displaystyle Q_{j,{\mathbf{a}}}^{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}/t_{J-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}m{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{*}_{i}{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}^{*}_{i}}^{\top}/t_{J-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}|}m\begin{pmatrix}\kappa_{j-1}/t_{J-1}&\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}}^{\top}/t_{J-1}\\
\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}}/t_{J-1}&\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}}^{\top}/t_{J-1}\end{pmatrix}.

Note that
{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}
and
{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}^{\top}
are both continuous, bounded functions of
{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}
because
\mathcal{X}
is bounded (as previously mentioned). By Theorem
11.2
, as
J\to\infty
,

\begin{pmatrix}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\kappa_{j-1}/t_{J-1}&\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}}^{\top}/t_{J-1}\\
\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}}/t_{J-1}&\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}}^{\top}/t_{J-1}\end{pmatrix}\overset{p}{\to}\mathscr{I}C^{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}_{*},

where
C_{*}^{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}
is a constant matrix. Additionally,

Q_{j,{\mathbf{a}}}^{{\mathbf{a}}}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}|}m\kappa_{j-1}/t_{J-1}\overset{p}{\to}\frac{m}{|\mathcal{A}|}\mathscr{I}.

Consequently, we know that

\displaystyle{\Sigma_{J}}^{-1/2}\eta_{J}^{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}{\Sigma_{J}}^{-1/2}=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathrm{diag}\left[\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\pi}}}\left\{\begin{pmatrix}{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{j}^{{\mathbf{a}}}&2{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{j}^{{\mathbf{a}}}{G^{*}_{{\mathbf{a}}}}^{\top}\\
2G^{*}_{{\mathbf{a}}}{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{j}^{{\mathbf{a}}}&4G^{*}_{{\mathbf{a}}}{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{j}^{{\mathbf{a}}}{G^{*}_{{\mathbf{a}}}}^{\top}+2n_{j}^{{\mathbf{a}}}\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{*}\end{pmatrix}\otimes\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}^{*}\mid\mathcal{F}_{j-1}\right\}_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}\right]/t_{J-1}\overset{p}{\to}C^{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}_{*}\mathscr{I}

as
J\to\infty
.

Lemma 11.8
.

Under conditions (1)-(6), for any covariate parameters
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}=\left\{\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}},G_{{\mathbf{a}}}\right\}_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}\in\mathcal{B}
and some
\delta_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}>0
,

\displaystyle\sigma_{\min}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\pi}}}\left[\mathrm{diag}\left\{\begin{pmatrix}{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{j}^{{\mathbf{a}}}&2{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{j}^{{\mathbf{a}}}{G_{{\mathbf{a}}}}^{\top}\\
2G_{{\mathbf{a}}}{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{j}^{{\mathbf{a}}}&4G_{{\mathbf{a}}}{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{j}^{{\mathbf{a}}}{G_{{\mathbf{a}}}}^{\top}+2n_{j}^{{\mathbf{a}}}\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}\end{pmatrix}\otimes\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}\right\}_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}/t_{J-1}\mid\mathcal{F}_{j-1}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\geq\delta_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}}\ \ \mathrm{w.p.\ 1}

Proof.

By Lemma
11.4
, we know that

\displaystyle\lim_{J\to\infty}\sigma_{\min}\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{J}\left\{\begin{pmatrix}Q_{j,{\mathbf{a}}}^{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}&2Q_{j,{\mathbf{a}}}^{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}{G_{{\mathbf{a}}}}^{\top}\\
2G_{{\mathbf{a}}}Q_{j,{\mathbf{a}}}^{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}&4G_{{\mathbf{a}}}Q_{j,{\mathbf{a}}}^{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}{G_{{\mathbf{a}}}}^{\top}+2Q_{j,{\mathbf{a}}}^{{\mathbf{a}}}\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}\end{pmatrix}\otimes\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}\right\}/t_{J-1}\right\}\geq

\displaystyle\lim_{J\to\infty}\sigma_{\min}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{J}Q_{j,{\mathbf{a}}}^{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}/t_{J-1}\right)\sigma_{\min}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{J}2Q_{j,{\mathbf{a}}}^{{\mathbf{a}}}\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}/t_{J-1}\right)

because

\lim_{J\to\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\left\{4G_{{\mathbf{a}}}Q_{j,{\mathbf{a}}}^{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}{G_{{\mathbf{a}}}}^{\top}+2Q_{j,{\mathbf{a}}}^{{\mathbf{a}}}\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}-4G_{{\mathbf{a}}}Q_{j,{\mathbf{a}}}^{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}{Q_{j,{\mathbf{a}}}^{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}}^{-1}Q_{j,{\mathbf{a}}}^{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}{G_{{\mathbf{a}}}}^{\top}\right\}/t_{J-1}=\lim_{J\to\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{J}2Q_{j,{\mathbf{a}}}^{{\mathbf{a}}}\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}/t_{J-1}.

We know that
\lim_{J\to\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{J}2Q_{j,{\mathbf{a}}}^{{\mathbf{a}}}\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}/t_{J-1}=2m\mathscr{I}\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}/\left|\mathcal{A}\right|
a.s., so we only need to prove that

\ \sum_{j=1}^{J}Q_{j,{\mathbf{a}}}^{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}/t_{J-1}\overset{p}{\to}\frac{m\mathscr{I}}{|\mathcal{A}|}\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left({\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{i}\right)^{\top}\\
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left({\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{i}\right)^{\top}&\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left({\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{i}{\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{i}^{\top}\right)\end{pmatrix}

is positive definite. By a second application of Lemma
11.4
,

\displaystyle\frac{m\mathscr{I}}{|\mathcal{A}|}\sigma_{\min}\left\{\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left({\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{i}\right)^{\top}\\
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left({\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{i}\right)&\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left({\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{i}{\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{i}^{\top}\right)\end{pmatrix}\right\}

\displaystyle\frac{m\mathscr{I}}{|\mathcal{A}|}\geq\sigma_{\min}\left(1\right)\times

\displaystyle\hskip 36.98866pt\sigma_{\min}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left({\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{i}{\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{i}^{\top}\right)-\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left({\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{i}\right)\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left({\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{i}\right)^{\top}\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\frac{m\mathscr{I}}{|\mathcal{A}|}\sigma_{\min}\left\{\mathrm{Var}_{\mu}\left({\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{i}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\succeq\frac{m\mathscr{I}}{|\mathcal{A}|}\sigma_{\min}\left(\Sigma_{{\mbox{\boldmath$k$}}}\right).

Note that there exists
\delta_{*}
such that
\mathscr{I}>\delta_{*}
by assumption. Using the properties of kronecker products, we know that defining

\epsilon_{{\mathbf{a}}}\triangleq 2\frac{m^{2}\delta_{*}^{2}}{|\mathcal{A}|^{2}}\sigma_{\min}\left(\Sigma_{{\mbox{\boldmath$k$}}}\right)\sigma_{\min}\left(\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}\right)^{2},

and

\delta_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}}\triangleq\prod_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}\epsilon_{{\mathbf{a}}}

completes the proof.

\blacksquare

By Lemma
11.8
,

\sigma_{\min}\left(\mathscr{I}C^{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}_{*}\right)\geq\delta_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}}
.
Consequently, Assumption
11
is satisfied for the covariate model.

Next, we analyze the reward model component,
\eta_{J}^{y}
.

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}\ell_{J}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y})}{\partial{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y}\partial{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y}^{\top}}=-\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j}}

\displaystyle{\mbox{\boldmath$Z$}}_{i}{{\mbox{\boldmath$Z$}}_{i}}^{\top}\left\{\frac{1}{1+\exp(-{{\mbox{\boldmath$Z$}}_{i}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y})}\right\}\left\{\frac{1}{1+\exp\left({{\mbox{\boldmath$Z$}}_{i}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y}\right)}\right\}.

Remember that
{\mbox{\boldmath$Z$}}_{i}=\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}){\mathbb{I}}\left({\mathbf{A}}_{r^{i}}={\mathbf{a}}\right)
, where
h_{{\mathbf{a}}}
is a continuous function in
{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}
that depends on
{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}
. Consequently,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}\ell_{J}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y})}{\partial{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y}\partial{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y}^{\top}}=

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j}}\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})^{\top}\left\{\frac{1}{1+\exp(-h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y})}\right\}\times

\displaystyle\left\{\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y}\right)}\right\}{\mathbb{I}}\left({\mathbf{A}}_{r^{i}}={\mathbf{a}}\right).

We now express

\displaystyle\eta^{y}_{J}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbb{E}\Bigg\{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j}}\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})^{\top}\times

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\left\{\frac{1}{1+\exp(-h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}_{y})}\right\}\left\{\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}_{y}\right)}\right\}\times

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt{\mathbb{I}}\left({\mathbf{A}}_{r^{i}}={\mathbf{a}}\right)\mid\mathcal{F}_{j-1}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbb{E}\Bigg\{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j}}\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}\sum_{l\in\mathbb{G}_{1}}\mathbb{I}(il\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*})h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})^{\top}\times

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\left\{\frac{1}{1+\exp(-h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}_{y})}\right\}\left\{\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}_{y}\right)}\right\}\times

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt{\mathbb{I}}\left({\mathbf{A}}_{r^{i}}={\mathbf{a}}\right)\mid\mathcal{F}_{j-1}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}\sum_{l\in\mathbb{G}_{1}}\mathbb{E}\Bigg\{\mathbb{I}(il\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*})\mathbb{E}\Bigg\{\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il})h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il})^{\top}\left\{\frac{1}{1+\exp(-h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}_{y})}\right\}\times

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\left\{\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}_{y}\right)}\right\}{\mathbb{I}}\left({\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}}\right)\mid{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i},\ il\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*}\Bigg\}\mid{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}\sum_{l\in\mathbb{G}_{1}}\mathbb{E}\Bigg\{\mathbb{I}(il\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*})\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}|}\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}\mathbb{E}\Bigg\{h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il})h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il})^{\top}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\left\{\frac{1}{1+\exp(-h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}_{y})}\right\}\times

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\left\{\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}_{y}\right)}\right\}{\mathbb{I}}\left({\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}}\right)\mid{\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}}^{\prime},{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i},\ il\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*}\Bigg\}\mid{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}\sum_{l\in\mathbb{G}_{1}}\mathbb{E}\Bigg\{\mathbb{I}(il\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*})\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}|}\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}\mathbb{E}\Bigg\{h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il})h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il})^{\top}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\left\{\frac{1}{1+\exp(-h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}_{y})}\right\}\times

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\left\{\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}_{y}\right)}\right\}\mid{\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}},{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i},\ il\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*}\Bigg\}\mid{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}\Bigg\}

We label

\displaystyle f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il})=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}|}\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}\mathbb{E}\Bigg\{h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il})h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il})^{\top}\left\{\frac{1}{1+\exp(-h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}_{y})}\right\}\times

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\left\{\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}_{y}\right)}\right\}\mid{\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}},{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}\Bigg\},

where
f
is continuous because
h_{{\mathbf{a}}}
is continuous (and it is a composition of continuous functions). Consequently,

\displaystyle\eta^{y}_{J}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}\sum_{l\in\mathbb{G}_{1}}\mathbb{E}\left\{\mathbb{I}(il\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*})f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il})\mid{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}LP_{1}^{*}\left\{f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})\right\}.

Lastly, we can define
f^{*}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})=LP_{1}^{*}\left\{f({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})\right\}
and

\displaystyle\eta_{J}^{y}=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}f^{*}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}).

Note that
f^{*}
is continuous and bounded since compositions and integrals of continuous functions are continuous.
It is now clear that

\displaystyle\eta_{J}^{y}/t_{J-1}=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}f^{*}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})/t_{J-1}\overset{p}{\to}\mathscr{I}C_{*}^{y},

where
C_{*}^{y}
is a constant matrix.

Lemma 11.9
.

Under conditions (1)-(6), for any
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y}\in\mathcal{B}
, there exists
\delta_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y}}
such that

\displaystyle\lim_{J\to\infty}\sigma_{\min}\Bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbb{E}\Bigg\{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j}}\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})^{\top}\left\{\frac{1}{1+\exp(-h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y})}\right\}\times

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\left\{\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y}\right)}\right\}\times

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt{\mathbb{I}}\left({\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}}\right)\mid\mathcal{F}_{j-1}\Bigg\}/t_{J-1}\Bigg)\geq\delta_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y}}\ \ \mathrm{w.p.\ 1}

Proof.

Label

\displaystyle\sigma_{y}

\displaystyle=\lim_{J\to\infty}\sigma_{\min}\Bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbb{E}\Bigg\{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j}}\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})^{\top}\left\{\frac{1}{1+\exp(-h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y})}\right\}\times

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\left\{\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y}\right)}\right\}\times

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt{\mathbb{I}}\left({\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}}\right)\mid\mathcal{F}_{j-1}\Bigg\}\Bigg).

We start with analyzing the quantity

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in\mathbb{G}_{1}}\mathbb{E}\Bigg\{\mathbb{I}(il\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*})\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}|}\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}\mathbb{E}\Bigg\{h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il})h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il})^{\top}\left\{\frac{1}{1+\exp(-h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y})}\right\}\times

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\left\{\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y}\right)}\right\}\mid il\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*},{\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}},{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}\Bigg\}\mid{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}\Bigg\},

Because
\mathcal{X}
is compact and
h_{{\mathbf{a}}}
is continuous, we know that

c_{\exp}\triangleq\min_{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\in\mathcal{X}}\min_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}\left\{\frac{1}{1+\exp(-h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y})}\right\}\left\{\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y}\right)}\right\},

where
c_{\exp}>0
by the extreme value theorem. Consequently,

\displaystyle\succ c_{\exp}\sum_{l\in\mathbb{G}_{1}}\mathbb{E}\Bigg\{\mathbb{I}(il\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*})\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}|}\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}\mathbb{E}\Bigg\{h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il})h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il})^{\top}\mid{\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}},{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i},il\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*}\Bigg\}\mid{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}\Bigg\}.

To progress we need to be more explicit about
h_{{\mathbf{a}}}
. In our model
Z_{i}
is an interaction between
X_{i}
and an indicator of the coupon type given to pariticipant
i
. We demonstrate the positive definite property for a paradigm where we have two coupon types
\mathcal{A}=\{{\mathbf{a}},{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}\}
, making
{\mbox{\boldmath$Z$}}_{i}=\left(1,{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i},{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}\mathbb{I}({\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}})\right)
. However, this logic extends to a larger number of coupon types. We find that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}|}\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}\mathbb{E}\Bigg\{{\mbox{\boldmath$Z$}}_{il}{\mbox{\boldmath$Z$}}_{il}^{\top}\mid il\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*},{\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}},{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}|}\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}\mathbb{E}\Bigg\{{\mbox{\boldmath$Z$}}_{il}{\mbox{\boldmath$Z$}}_{il}^{\top}\mid{\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}},{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left\{\begin{pmatrix}1&{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il}^{\top}&0\\
{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il}^{\top}&{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il}{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il}^{\top}&0\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix}|{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i},{\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\mathbb{E}\left\{\begin{pmatrix}1&{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il}^{\top}&{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il}^{\top}\\
{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il}^{\top}&{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il}{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il}^{\top}&{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il}{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il}^{\top}\\
{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il}}^{\top}&{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il}{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il}^{\top}&{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il}{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il}}^{\top}\end{pmatrix}|{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i},{\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}}\right\}.

We label
\mu_{{\mathbf{a}},\bf{x}}=\mathbb{E}\left\{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il}|{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}=\bf{x},{\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}}\right\}
and
\Delta_{{\mathbf{a}}}=\mathbb{E}\left\{{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il}{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il}^{\top}|{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}={\mbox{\boldmath$x$}},{\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}}\right\}
. Note that
\Delta_{{\mathbf{a}}}
does not depend on
{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}
. We find that

\displaystyle\Delta_{0}\triangleq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}|}\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}\mathbb{E}\Bigg\{{\mbox{\boldmath$Z$}}_{il}{\mbox{\boldmath$Z$}}_{il}^{\top}\mid{\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}},{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}=\bf{x}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&\mu_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime},\bf{x}}^{\top}&0\\
\mu_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime},\bf{x}}&\Delta_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}&0\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}1&\mu_{{\mathbf{a}},\bf{x}}^{\top}&\mu_{{\mathbf{a}},\bf{x}}^{\top}\\
\mu_{{\mathbf{a}},\bf{x}}&\Delta_{{\mathbf{a}}}&\Delta_{{\mathbf{a}}}\\
\mu_{{\mathbf{a}},\bf{x}}&\Delta_{{\mathbf{a}}}&\Delta_{{\mathbf{a}}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}2&\mu_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime},\bf{x}}^{\top}+\mu_{{\mathbf{a}},\bf{x}}^{\top}&\mu_{{\mathbf{a}},\bf{x}}^{\top}\\
\mu_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime},\bf{x}}+\mu_{{\mathbf{a}},\bf{x}}&\Delta_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}+\Delta_{{\mathbf{a}}}&\Delta_{{\mathbf{a}}}\\
\mu_{{\mathbf{a}},\bf{x}}&\Delta_{{\mathbf{a}}}&\Delta_{{\mathbf{a}}}\end{pmatrix}.

By Lemma
11.4
, we know that
\sigma_{\min}\left(\Delta_{0}\right)\geq 2\sigma_{\min}(\Delta_{1})
, where

\displaystyle\Delta_{1}

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{pmatrix}\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}+\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}+\frac{(\mu_{{\mathbf{a}},\bf{x}}-\mu_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime},\bf{x}})(\mu_{{\mathbf{a}},\bf{x}}-\mu_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime},\bf{x}})^{\top}}{2}&\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}+\frac{(\mu_{{\mathbf{a}},\bf{x}}-\mu_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime},\bf{x}}){\mu_{{\mathbf{a}},\bf{x}}}^{\top}}{2}\\
\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}+\frac{\mu_{{\mathbf{a}},\bf{x}}(\mu_{{\mathbf{a}},\bf{x}}-\mu_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime},\bf{x}})^{\top}}{2}&\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}+\frac{\mu_{{\mathbf{a}},\bf{x}}{\mu_{{\mathbf{a}},\bf{x}}}^{\top}}{2}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}+\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}&\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}\\
\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}&\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\frac{(\mu_{{\mathbf{a}},\bf{x}}-\mu_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime},\bf{x}})(\mu_{{\mathbf{a}},\bf{x}}-\mu_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime},\bf{x}})^{\top}}{2}&\frac{(\mu_{{\mathbf{a}},\bf{x}}-\mu_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime},\bf{x}}){\mu_{{\mathbf{a}},\bf{x}}}^{\top}}{2}\\
\frac{\mu_{{\mathbf{a}},\bf{x}}(\mu_{{\mathbf{a}},\bf{x}}-\mu_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime},\bf{x}})^{\top}}{2}&\frac{\mu_{{\mathbf{a}},\bf{x}}{\mu_{{\mathbf{a}},\bf{x}}}^{\top}}{2}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\succeq\begin{pmatrix}\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}+\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}&\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}\\
\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}&\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}\end{pmatrix}.

We label

\Delta_{2}\triangleq\begin{pmatrix}\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}+\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}&\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}\\
\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}&\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}\end{pmatrix}.

By Lemma
11.4
again, we find that

\displaystyle\sigma_{\min}\left\{\Delta_{2}\right\}

\displaystyle\geq\sigma_{\min}(\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}})\sigma_{\min}(\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}+\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}-\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}{\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}}}^{-1}\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\min}(\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}})\sigma_{\min}(\Sigma_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}})>0.

We can conclude that

\displaystyle c_{\exp}\sum_{l\in\mathbb{G}_{1}}\mathbb{E}\Bigg\{\mathbb{I}(il\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*})\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}|}\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}}\mathbb{E}\Bigg\{h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il})h_{{\mathbf{a}}}({\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{il})^{\top}\mid{\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{a}},{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}\Bigg\}\mid{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\succ c_{\exp}\sum_{l\in\mathbb{G}_{1}}\mathbb{E}\Bigg\{\mathbb{I}(il\in{\mathbb{T}}^{*})\Delta_{2}\mid{\mbox{\boldmath$X$}}_{i}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\succ c_{\exp}m\Delta_{2}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\sigma_{y}

\displaystyle\geq\lim_{J\to\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}\frac{1}{t_{J-1}}c_{\exp}m\sigma_{\min}\left(\Delta_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\lim_{J\to\infty}\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{J}}{t_{J-1}}c_{\exp}m\sigma_{\min}\left(\Delta_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathscr{I}c_{\exp}m\sigma_{\min}\left(\Delta_{2}\right).

Note that there exists
\delta_{*}
such that
\mathscr{I}>\delta_{*}
by assumption. Defining

\delta_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{y}}\triangleq\delta^{*}c_{\exp}m\sigma_{\min}\left(\Delta_{2}\right)

completes the proof.

\blacksquare

We conclude that
\sigma_{\min}\left(\mathscr{I}C_{*}^{y}\right)\geq\delta_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}_{y}}
and we can conclude
Assumption
11
is satisfied for the reward model component of the branching process.

Next, we analyze the time model component,
\eta_{J}^{t}
.

The Hessian for this part of the model is

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}\ell_{J}(\zeta)}{\partial\zeta^{2}}=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}-M_{i}\left(\frac{1}{\zeta^{2}}+\frac{ae^{-\zeta a}-b^{2}e^{-\zeta b}}{[e^{-\zeta a}-e^{-\zeta b}]^{2}}\right).

We get that

\displaystyle\lim_{J\to\infty}\eta_{J}^{t}/t_{J-1}

\displaystyle=\lim_{J\to\infty}\overline{\kappa}_{J}/t_{J-1}\left(\frac{1}{{\zeta^{*}}^{2}}+\frac{ae^{-{\zeta^{*}}a}-b^{2}e^{-{\zeta^{*}}b}}{[e^{-{\zeta^{*}}a}-e^{-{\zeta^{*}}b}]^{2}}\right)m

\displaystyle=\mathscr{I}\left(\frac{1}{{\zeta^{*}}^{2}}+\frac{ae^{-{\zeta^{*}}a}-b^{2}e^{-{\zeta^{*}}b}}{[e^{-{\zeta^{*}}a}-e^{-{\zeta^{*}}b}]^{2}}\right)m

\displaystyle>0.

Where the last inequality follows because there exists
\delta_{*}
such that
\mathscr{I}>\delta_{*}
by assumption. We can conclude
Assumption
11
is satisfied for the time model component of the branching process.

Lastly, we analyze the family size model component,
\eta_{J}^{m}
.

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}\ell_{J}(\tau)}{\partial\tau^{2}}=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}-\tau^{2}\frac{\left\{\sum_{\ell=k}^{|\mathbf{A}_{i}|}e^{\tau\ell}/\ell!\right\}\left\{\sum_{\ell=k}^{|\mathbf{A}_{i}|}\ell^{2}e^{\tau\ell}/\ell!\right\}-\left\{\sum_{\ell=k}^{|\mathbf{A}_{i}|}\ell e^{\tau\ell}/\ell!\right\}^{2}}{\left\{\sum_{\ell=k}^{|\mathbf{A}_{i}|}e^{\tau\ell}/\ell!\right\}^{2}}.

We find

\displaystyle\lim_{J\to\infty}\eta_{J}/t_{J-1}

\displaystyle=\lim_{J\to\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}{\tau^{*}}^{2}\frac{\left\{\sum_{\ell=k}^{|\mathbf{A}_{i}|}e^{{\tau^{*}}\ell}/\ell!\right\}\left\{\sum_{\ell=k}^{|\mathbf{A}_{i}|}\ell^{2}e^{{\tau^{*}}\ell}/\ell!\right\}-\left\{\sum_{\ell=k}^{|\mathbf{A}_{i}|}\ell e^{{\tau^{*}}\ell}/\ell!\right\}^{2}}{\left\{\sum_{\ell=k}^{|\mathbf{A}_{i}|}e^{{\tau^{*}}\ell}/\ell!\right\}^{2}}/t_{J-1}

\displaystyle=\lim_{J\to\infty}\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{J}}{t_{J-1}}{\tau^{*}}^{2}\frac{\left\{\sum_{\ell=k}^{L}e^{{\tau^{*}}\ell}/\ell!\right\}\left\{\sum_{\ell=k}^{L}\ell^{2}e^{{\tau^{*}}\ell}/\ell!\right\}-\left\{\sum_{\ell=k}^{L}\ell e^{{\tau^{*}}\ell}/\ell!\right\}^{2}}{\left\{\sum_{\ell=k}^{L}e^{{\tau^{*}}\ell}/\ell!\right\}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{I}{\tau^{*}}^{2}\frac{\left\{\sum_{\ell=k}^{L}e^{{\tau^{*}}\ell}/\ell!\right\}\left\{\sum_{\ell=k}^{L}\ell^{2}e^{{\tau^{*}}\ell}/\ell!\right\}-\left\{\sum_{\ell=k}^{L}\ell e^{{\tau^{*}}\ell}/\ell!\right\}^{2}}{\left\{\sum_{\ell=k}^{L}e^{{\tau^{*}}\ell}/\ell!\right\}^{2}}

Note that there exists
\delta_{*}
such that
\mathscr{I}>\delta_{*}
by assumption. We can conclude
Assumption
11
is satisfied for the family model component of the branching process.

In conclusion, we have confirmed that

\lim_{J\to\infty}\eta_{J}/t_{J-1}=\mathscr{I}\mathbb{C}

where
\mathscr{I}\mathbb{C}\succ 0
w.p. 1 on the event
E_{\mathscr{I}}
. We establish one more lemma that will be useful in the consistency proof of Section
11.4
.

Lemma 11.10
.

Recall in the model described in Equation
6
,
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t}\triangleq\zeta
and
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{m}\triangleq\tau
. Under conditions (1)-(6), for any
\zeta,\tau\in\mathcal{B}
, there exists
\delta_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t}},\delta_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{m}}>0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{J\to\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}M_{i}\left(\frac{1}{\zeta^{2}}+\frac{ae^{-\zeta a}-b^{2}e^{-\zeta b}}{[e^{-\zeta a}-e^{-\zeta b}]^{2}}\right)\mid\mathcal{F}_{j-1}\right]/t_{J-1}\geq\delta_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t}}\ \mathrm{a.s.}

\displaystyle\lim_{J\to\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbb{E}\left\{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}_{j-1}}{\tau}^{2}\frac{\left\{\sum_{\ell=k}^{|\mathbf{A}_{i}|}e^{{\tau}\ell}/\ell!\right\}\left\{\sum_{\ell=k}^{|\mathbf{A}_{i}|}\ell^{2}e^{{\tau}\ell}/\ell!\right\}-\left\{\sum_{\ell=k}^{|\mathbf{A}_{i}|}\ell e^{{\tau}\ell}/\ell!\right\}^{2}}{\left\{\sum_{\ell=k}^{|\mathbf{A}_{i}|}e^{{\tau}\ell}/\ell!\right\}^{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{j-1}\right\}/t_{J-1}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\geq\delta_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{m}}\ \mathrm{a.s.}

on event
E_{\mathscr{I}}
.

Proof.

For any
\zeta\in\mathcal{B}
,
